# PREFACE.

MEN can be divided into two general classes <note>original: "Ordres"</note>. *There are those who have no knowledge of the Divinity, nor any sense of Religion. Travelers report that in the West Indies <note>A term used at the time to refer to the Americas.</note>, in Eastern Tartary <note>Historical term for the region of Manchuria and parts of East Asia.</note>, and in some places in Africa, there are certain peoples who are in this state.* I confess that this is a fact contested by others, and that *Fabricius <note>Likely Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific German scholar and theologian known for his bibliographies and works on Christian apologetics.</note>, a theologian of the Palatinate*, claims to have solidly refuted it. If this fact is considered doubtful, it is at least known that here and there in the world, some individuals are found who treat the existence of a Divinity as a fable, and who mock the acts of Religion, whatever it may be. But it can also be said that if they are compared with the rest of the world, they are but the smallest and the

# PREFACE.

the most wicked part of the human race, even though many of these people affect an appearance of living morally well.

All the rest of the world professes to recognize that there is a God—that is to say, a most perfect, Eternal, and Independent Being; that God governs all things through his *Providence*; that there is a difference between good and evil; and that Man performs good or evil through the acts of his liberty. They equally conclude from this that Man is born for the duties of Religion, and that without it, Society would be nothing but pure brigandage <note>The author uses this term to describe a state of lawless plunder or total social collapse.</note>; and since it is difficult to conceive that Men perish entirely at death, it is also directly contrary to the sentiments of Conscience to conceive of neither rewards for virtue nor punishments for vice after the course of this life.

Whatever diversity of Religions may be found among Men, they can be reduced to two types: The first consists of those who conceive that there is more than one God: it is in this rank that all the *Pagans* belong.

The second consists of those who recognize only one Divinity, such as the *Jews*, the *Christians*, and the *Mohamedans* <note>A common historical term used in the West at this time to refer to Muslims.</note>.

Just as to cure different diseases one must use different remedies, it

# PREFACE.

is visible that to deliver Men from their different prejudices, one must follow very different methods.

The ignorance of those barbaric peoples of the Indies, of Tartary <note>A historical term used by Europeans from the Middle Ages to the 20th century to describe a vast region of North and Central Asia.</note>, and of Africa, requires that they be instructed in the first principles, and that they be made to apply to the capital Maxims of Religion the few sentiments they have left regarding the just and the unjust, the honorable and the dishonorable.

One must convince reflective Atheists by principles they admit, by showing that the truths they reject are the natural consequences of the principles they dare not contest.

To convince the Pagans who conceive of several Divinities, it is necessary to examine their principles and refute them, which is the easiest thing in the world; the wisest having formerly recognized, and still recognizing today, that there is only one God. Indeed, it seems that most Philosophers <note>original: "Filosofes"</note> only made a profession of believing in a plurality of Gods to accommodate themselves to the sentiments of the People, whom it was dangerous to offend.

With regard to the Jews, as they agree on the Unity of God with Christians and Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note>, it is only a matter of their

# P R E F A C E.

can be true, without all the other religions being false in those points where they essentially differ from the Christian Religion.

And on the other hand, one cannot explain the evidence that solidly establishes the truth of Christianity without proving other religions to be false, because they lack those proofs which are unique to the Christian Religion.

I am well aware that there are various paths leading to the goal I have set for myself. Those with a meditative mind desire that, through reflections on the human heart and its feelings, one might establish the perfect conformity of the Christian Religion with the human Conscience <term>Conscience: Here referring to the internal moral sense and innate knowledge of right and wrong.</term>, which provides them with a convincing proof and a demonstration.

Others focus on examining Dogmas <term>Dogmas: The core, established principles and doctrines of the faith.</term> and the evidence of the Christian Religion in a speculative <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, "speculative" referred to theoretical or philosophical reasoning as opposed to practical or historical observation.</note> manner, in order to show their conformity with the ideas of reason. I do not wish to deny that they employ their reflections usefully in this type of study, nor that truth finds great assistance in their meditations on this subject.

But whatever advantage truth may gain from this, I have nevertheless preferred to follow another path, and I have found it more useful to solidly establish the facts upon which Religion

# PREFACE

Christianity proposes, which seemed to me more capable of persuading all sorts of readers, and more suited to the ordinary reach of those who first enter into the examination of this truth.

As the necessity of Revelation <term>Revelation: The communication of divine truth or knowledge from God to humanity, often through scripture or prophecy.</term> is a fact generally admitted by Pagans <term>Pagans: A term historically used by Christian authors to refer to those who practiced polytheistic or non-Abrahamic religions.</term> and all other peoples of the world, I believed it was useless to enter into the examination of certain abstract questions, such as these: whether there is a natural knowledge of God, whether men are naturally inclined toward Religion, and other similar matters. When I have solidly proven that God revealed himself and prescribed a service <note>original: "Service"; in this context, it refers to a formal system of worship or religious duty.</note> to the first men—who left the rules for it to all their descendants, from which has come everything that has been and still is of Religion in the Pagan world—I will have avoided many very useless disputes that can only confuse the mind through their obscurity.

I have therefore confined myself to certain considerations which so establish the truth of the Books of the Old and New Testaments that, through their union, they very solidly prove the truth of the Christian Religion.

After this, I hope that it will not be

# CHAPTER I.
### *On the Foundations of the Christian Religion.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'O' set in a square frame with floral ornamentation.</image-desc>
ONE must consider three things in the Christian Religion: the Facts which it proposes to us as true; the Promises it makes to us; and the Worship <note>original: "Culte." In a 17th-century context, this refers to the external expression of religion, including its rites, ceremonies, and systems of prayer.</note> which it commands of us.
The Facts which it proposes to us as true are that God created the World, that He

<term>Christian Religion</term>: The system of faith and practice based on the Old and New Testaments.
<term>Holy Scripture</term>: The collective sacred texts of the Bible.
<term>Foundations</term>: The underlying principles or facts upon which the religion is built.
<term>Facts</term>: Historical or cosmic events asserted as true by the scriptures.
<term>Promises</term>: Divine assurances of future rewards or outcomes.
<term>Worship</term>: The specific duties and rituals owed to the Divine.
<term>World</term>: The created universe.

<summary>This opening page of the treatise establishes the three pillars of the Christian Religion: historical facts, divine promises, and mandated worship. It begins its argument by asserting that God is the creator of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, Holy Scripture, Creation, Foundations of Faith, Worship, Divine Promises</keywords>

It is, I say, impossible to regard those Facts as true without being assured that God will fulfill the Promises he has made to us.

It is no less evident that one cannot be convinced of the truth of those Facts without finding oneself committed to fulfilling the duties that the Christian Religion imposes on us.

From the very moment that a Man regards himself as God’s Creature <note>In 17th-century terms, "Creature" refers to any created being, emphasizing the relationship between the maker (God) and the made (Man).</note>, he naturally owes God all the obedience of which he is capable. But when he comes to believe that God did not cause the first Man to perish after his sin, but that He was willing to promise him a Savior, for himself and for all his Posterity; when, moreover, he regards it as a truth that God has indeed sent this Savior in the manner that the Gospel describes to us; then it is even more impossible that Man should not render religious obedience to God, and that he should not put faith in all His Promises.

Man is bound by a second tie to obey God, to pray to Him, and to render Him every kind of religious service. God, through the Redemption <term>Redemption</term> <note>original: "Rédemption." The theological concept of humanity being "bought back" or saved from the consequences of sin through the sacrifice of Jesus Christ.</note>, has acquired a new right over Man; Man has entered into a stronger obligation to submit to God through the Acts of Religion; He has not only

## CHAP. II.

### *That the Christian Religion is founded upon proofs of fact.*

In our effort to establish the truth of the Christian Religion, we are presently narrowing our focus to those proofs that demonstrate the truth of the facts it presents, setting aside other types of evidence even though they are no less compelling. I maintain that it is evident that the proofs we must produce to support the facts in question must be of the same nature as the proofs used to establish any other facts.

If we were dealing with events that had occurred in our own time, it would be right to produce eyewitnesses. However, as we are dealing with events far removed from the century in which we live, it is only natural to establish them through the testimony of historians who relay the depositions <note>original: "déposition." In this context, it refers to the formal statements or accounts given by witnesses.</note> of eyewitnesses. This is the singular means by which one establishes the credibility of ancient events.

I only know that there once existed a Cyrus, an Alexander, or a Caesar <note>The author cites these three famous figures—Cyrus the Great of Persia, Alexander the Great of Macedon, and Julius Caesar of Rome—as examples of historical truths that no reasonable person doubts, despite them living centuries ago.</note> through the testimony of historians; and yet, if I have read the history

<meta>catchword: history</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the truth of Christianity should be evaluated using historical standards of evidence, specifically the testimony of historians who recorded eyewitness accounts of ancient events.</summary>
<keywords>Christian Religion, historical evidence, eyewitnesses, ancient history, Cyrus, Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, credibility of facts</keywords>

history, these are facts of which I cannot reasonably doubt.

I admit that the certainty we possess regarding facts that occurred in a time distant from our own is not as great as that which we have for facts of which we have eyewitnesses. Nevertheless, as it is known that one cannot establish the truth of facts distant from our time except through proofs of this nature; no one has yet thought to dismiss the existence of Cyrus and Alexander <note>The author refers to Cyrus the Great (founder of the Persian Empire) and Alexander the Great (the Macedonian conqueror). These were standard examples in 18th-century philosophy for things we believe through "historical faith" rather than direct experience.</note> as fables, on the pretext that there is no longer any witness who saw them, and that there remains almost no trace of the Empires they founded. Indeed, the certainty we have of those facts is such that nothing can be added to it; the reason is that even though it is based on the testimony of historians who died several centuries ago, on one hand, these facts perfectly embody the idea of probability and truth, and on the other, these facts serve as the foundation for the entire history of the following centuries, which cannot be called into doubt if one considers its interconnectedness and links, according to the light of common sense and the equity of reason.

A fact is therefore considered established <note>original: "constant." In this period, "constant" meant a fact that was steady, undisputed, and proven beyond reasonable doubt.</note> when it is recounted by those who saw it, when it is written by an historian who lived among those who distinctly knew that fact, and when it is found to be contradicted by no one; when it was written in a time when

<summary>The author argues that while historical accounts are less certain than eyewitness testimony, we accept the existence of figures like Alexander the Great because their lives are logically linked to the subsequent flow of history. He defines an established historical fact as one written by contemporaries and left uncontradicted.</summary>

<keywords>historical certainty, eyewitness testimony, Cyrus the Great, Alexander the Great, historical methodology, common sense, evidence</keywords>

# GENESIS.

-ian where it was impossible to write things differently than they had occurred without exposing oneself to public ridicule. Finally, when it appears that the fact in question was of such a nature that it could not be unknown to anyone, that everyone had an interest in inquiring about it, that indeed everyone was precisely informed of it; and lastly, that it possessed a natural connection with all the events that necessarily depended upon it.

At its core, it is not just to demand a greater number of proofs, or proofs of a different nature, for the establishment of the facts proposed by Religion, than those demanded to confirm the truth of any other fact one wishes to establish. Why should the testimony of the children of Noah <note>The sons of Noah (Shem, Ham, and Japheth) who, according to the Bible, survived the Great Flood.</note> not be sufficient to prove that there was a Methuselah, if they report that they saw Methuselah? And why would the testimony of Methuselah not suffice to persuade us that there was an Adam, if this Methuselah reports that he saw Adam? <note>The author is using a common 18th-century apologetic argument: because of the long lifespans recorded in Genesis, only one person (Methuselah) was needed to link the first man, Adam, to the generation of the Flood.</note> Do we not every day place faith in the account of an old man who gives the history of his predecessors, especially when it appears that what he recounts follows a very logical sequence and has a very precise connection with everything that appears before our eyes?

But it is easy to show that the proofs establishing the truth of the facts proposed by the Christian Religion are infinitely stronger and more evident.

These four remarks give roughly the entire idea of Holy Scripture, and hardly anything more is needed to demonstrate the truth of these Books, if one considers the incontestable facts that I am going to relate, and if one makes the reflections that can very naturally be made upon them.

The first Fact is, that Christians—who moreover were divided into various Parties <note>Referring to the early theological schisms and denominations in the first few centuries of Christianity.</note> almost immediately after Jesus Christ—have read, and constantly read in all places, every first Day of the Week <note>A reference to Sunday service.</note>, the Books of the New Testament, of which they have Versions in all sorts of Languages, so much so that it is absolutely impossible that any forgery <note>original: "suposition"; here meaning the fraudulent substitution or insertion of false texts.</note> could have occurred.

It is now time to enter into the subject matter, beginning with the Book of Genesis, which relates the facts of the Creation of the World, the manner in which Man was formed, his fall, the promise of the Messiah <note>The "promise of the Messiah" refers to the theological interpretation of Genesis 3:15, often called the *Protevangelium* or the "first gospel," where God promises that the seed of the woman will crush the serpent's head.</note>, the propagation of the human race, the Flood, and other events until the death of Joseph; this encompasses the history of the world for a period of 2,369 years <note>The author is using a traditional biblical chronology, likely derived from the Masoretic text, which was common in 17th and 18th-century scholarship.</note>.

->*Regarding the Facts of the Creation of the World*<-

Here, then, is what I propose to establish these two facts: the first, that God created a

Moses, the **Lawgiver** <note>Original: *Legiſlateur*. A term commonly used in the 17th and 18th centuries to emphasize Moses's role not just as a prophet, but as the constitutional founder of the Hebrew state.</note> of the Jews, laid these two Foundations before publishing his Laws, and he describes the details of these Facts as things that were not only known to him alone, or to a few others of the Jewish People, but as Facts that were known to all the Peoples of the World, and particularly to the entire Nation in the midst of which he lived.

To feel the force of this proof, three things must be noted: one, the very nature of the Facts in question; another, the character of the person who wrote them; the third is the use that Moses made of these Facts which he recites.

Nothing should have been better known than these two Facts in question. These were matters that concerned everyone; they were Facts of which each person should have been informed; they were Facts about which it was not easy to deceive, since everyone could have easily learned the truth for themselves, and thus should have been very accurately informed.

I maintain that there are no Facts in **Secular History** <note>Original: *Hiſtoire Profane*. This refers to history written by non-biblical authors (Greeks, Romans, etc.), distinguished from "Sacred History" found in the Bible.</note> whose importance can be compared with the facts of the **Creation** and the **Promise** of the Messiah, as Moses describes them to us.

I further maintain that there is no Author whose Character is as Illustrious as that of Moses, and who has as many essential marks of fidelity and truth as he has shown in his writing.

However, since Moses intended to justify in the minds of his people both the plan he inspired in them to leave Egypt and the claims they held over the land of **Canaan** where he wished to lead them, it was natural to present them with the source of these claims. The greater part of this book concerns only the ancestors of the People of Israel, after Moses had explained the foundations of **Religion** and what was known to all Nations.

What can they reasonably object? They might say that **Moses** is not the author of the **Book of Genesis**, but that it was falsely attributed to him under his name; and thus everything built upon **Moses’s** authority and testimony lacks any solid foundation. They may object that even if **Moses** is the true author of the **Book of Genesis**, he was separated by many centuries from the events he recounts, which strips his history of its authority. They may also say that it is not generally supposed that **Moses** wrote these facts as a **Historian**, but as a **Prophet**, and that the common idea formed of his works is that he wrote about things that were—prior to him—very obscure, very confused, or even generally unknown.

At the very least, it is true that these **Facts** have given rise to most of the **Fables** <note>In this context, 'fables' refers to the myths and legends of the Greco-Roman world, which early modern scholars often viewed as corrupted versions of biblical history.</note>: to that of **Chaos**; to that of the **Marriage of Peleus & Thetis** for the formation of **Man**; to that of **Prometheus**; to that of the laughter of **Jupiter** during the first seven days of his life, which they regarded as the origin of the solemnity of the **seventh day** known among the **Pagans**; to that of the **Golden Age** <note>Commonly associated with the Garden of Eden.</note>; to that of the event of the **Flood** under the name of **Deucalion**; to the **Fable of Janus** <note>Many 17th-century scholars identified the two-faced Janus with Noah, who looked back at the world before the flood and forward to the world after.</note>; to that of the division of the **World** among the **Children of Saturn** <note>A parallel to the division of the earth between the three sons of Noah: Shem, Ham, and Japheth.</note>; to the name of **Jupiter Hammon** <note>Associated by the author with Ham, the son of Noah.</note>; to the **Fable of the Titans** <note>Linked to the giants of Genesis or the builders of the Tower of Babel.</note>; to the changing of **Women into stone Statues** <note>A reference to Lot’s wife being turned into a pillar of salt.</note>; to the tales of the **Incests of the Gods** with their **Daughters** <note>A reference to the story of Lot and his daughters.</note>; to that of the conflagration of the **World** under **Phaeton** <note>Likened to the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah by fire.</note>; & to a great number of others, which cannot be otherwise explained, as the **Scholars** have shown.

**26**

Whoever the Author of the book of **Genesis** may be, whether **Moses** or another, it remains a constant truth that he was accurately informed of the things he recounts, & that he lived shortly after **Joseph**.

Firstly, I say that he was very well informed of the **Facts** of which he speaks; He identifies by their names the Leaders of the various **Peoples** <note>original: "Peuplades". This likely refers to the "Table of Nations" in Genesis 10, which lists the descendants of Noah as the founders of various ancient civilizations.</note> of the **World**, and he does so in a manner that corresponds very well with what we find in the most ancient **Historians**: He speaks of these Peoples, of their States, of their Kings, as things that were equally known to him: He carefully distinguishes the origin of all these Peoples; and this origin being famous at that time, it could not have been represented otherwise by any Historian, without making a laughingstock of the one who might have wanted to mix the fabulous into accounts of this nature.

I have said in the second place, that this Book was written by a Man who lived shortly after **Joseph**; he speaks accurately of the entire **Ministry of Joseph**; in particular he shows the origin of the **Fifth-penny tax** <term>Fifth-penny tax (French: *Quint dénier*): This refers to the 20% land tax established by Joseph in Egypt during the seven years of famine, as recorded in Genesis 47.</term> that **Egypt** paid for so long to its Princes, which could not have been unknown to any of the **Egyptians**. Finally, it appears visibly that this Book serves as a preamble to the Book of **Exodus** & to the following ones; that the following books are founded solely upon the truth of **Genesis**; that they make perpetual allusions to it, and that the entire **Religion** established by this Book is founded on the truth of the facts of the **Creation** & of the **Promise of the Messiah**, which are explained in **Genesis**,

But I go even further, and I say that the Jews cannot have been mistaken in the testimony they give regarding this; here are two remarks that clearly demonstrate it.

The first is that their Civil and Religious observances are still today founded on the principles that one finds only in *Genesis*: For example, they count the beginning of the day from the evening <note>Based on the creation narrative in Genesis 1: "And there was evening and there was morning, the first day."</note>, they observe the *Sabbath*, they practice *Circumcision*, they abstain from eating the muscle which is at the socket of the hip <note>original: "muscle qui est à l'emboîtement de la hanche." The observance of these *Laws* is prescribed to them in the other Books that bear the name of Moses: But one finds neither the occasion nor the pretext for them except in the book of *Genesis*, to which these *Laws* have a necessary relationship.

The second is that the Book of *Genesis* assumes that the Posterity of *Abraham* as well as the Ancestors had constantly practiced the *Sabbath* and *Circumcision*; and subsequently the Books of Moses ordering that this Law be read continually in every Family—for which purpose every *Sabbath* day was even more naturally intended—and that it be read in its entirety every seventh year, as we know the Jews practiced by virtue of the *Laws* of Moses, they still practice the first in all places, and have only ceased to practice the second because

The deception <note>original: "suposition." Certainly, if it is possible for a fraud to make it believed that he has some communication with the *Divinity*, a People who are subject to his Laws can easily believe it: But it is not possible for a People to suddenly forget all the facts they learned from their *Ancestors*, & to receive in their place fables forged at will.

Peoples have sometimes been ridiculous enough to give themselves a fabulous *Origin*; but it has never been when speaking to them of a time that was close to their own.

One cannot prove more solidly that it is impossible for the fact of the Creation of the World to have been falsely asserted by Moses, than by showing that the Law of the Sabbath <term>Sabbath</term><note>The weekly day of rest; the author argues it serves as a living memorial of the six days of creation.</note> has a natural relation to the fact of Creation, and that it has always been observed constantly until Moses. This very point will serve to make one clearly understand in what manner the idea of the Promise of the Messiah <term>Messiah</term><note>The "anointed one" or savior promised in biblical prophecy.</note> was preserved very distinctly among those who lived from the beginning of the world until Moses. As soon as one knows that the Sabbath is a solemn day that returns every seven days, and in which it was commanded to celebrate the memory of the Creation of the World, one judges well enough that it is an entire impossibility that an event as important as that of the Creation of the World—of which a commemoration is made every seventh day by virtue of a Divine Law—could be a fabricated fact. 

But as this impossibility results from two things: one, that God gave the Law of the Sabbath to the first man <note>The "first man" refers to Adam, whom the author believes received the command to rest directly from God.</note>; the other, that it was observed by his descendants until the time of Moses, and that God did nothing but renew its concepts in the Law he gave at Sinai <note>Referring to Mount Sinai, where Moses received the Ten Commandments.</note>; it is right to solidly establish these truths. This is all the more important because if the multitude of ancient and modern authors, both Jewish and Christian, is

### EXODUS

[...assigned] a time proportioned to their joy. For if we see that the Egyptians dedicated ten weeks to the mourning of **Jacob**, on account of his rank, we know that **Joseph** and those who followed him gave seven days to it, when they went to bury him in the **Land of Canaan**; and if one sees that this custom was perpetuated among the **Jews**, as it appears in **Ecclesiasticus** <note>Also known as the Book of Sirach, a book of ethical teachings found in the Catholic and Orthodox bibles, and the Apocrypha of Protestant bibles.</note> chapter twenty-two, verse twelve, it also took place among the Asians, as appears in the History of **Ammianus Marcellinus** <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian who wrote about the later Roman Empire.</note> at the beginning of the nineteenth book, and it passed among the **Christians**, as is seen in **Saint Ambrose**.

Thirdly, it appears in the seventh chapter of **Exodus**, verse twenty-five, that God observed seven days from the time he struck the River to change its water into blood; from which the **Jews** conclude that the three Earthly Plagues—that of Blood, that of Frogs, and that of Lice—remained over Egypt for six days, and that they were withdrawn on the seventh, which was the day of the **Sabbath**.

### CHAPTER VIII.

#### *That Adam was convinced of his Creation by Reason and by Authority.*

It would perhaps be more natural, in the examination of the **History of Genesis**, to begin the discussion of the **Facts** that **Moses** recites with those that occurred in the times closest to **Moses**, in order to work back to those that are the most distant. Nevertheless, I prefer to follow the order that **Moses** followed, and I even hope that it will shed more light on these **Reflections**: at the very least, it will spare me the indispensable necessity I would otherwise face of falling into frequent repetitions. I begin, therefore, by establishing that **Adam** was convinced of his **Creation** by reason and by authority.

Since the **Creation of Adam** is a **Fact** of which he first had to be convinced before being able to persuade his descendants of its truth, it is important to first examine in what manner he was able to assure himself that the matter had occurred exactly as it is recited by **Moses** <note>The author is treating the biblical figure of Adam as a historical witness who needed to logically process his own miraculous origin.</note>. Indeed, since the certainty of this **Tradition** depends on the authority of **Adam**, whom **Moses** supposes to have been its first **Author**; it is clear that one must be assured that **Adam** could not

Regarding his own **Existence**, it is clear enough that it was known to **Adam**, just as our **Existence** is known to each of us. The manner in which he had received being is the most abstruse <term>abstruse: complex or difficult to understand; here referring to the mystery of life's origin</term> part of this matter: But he could learn about it through two ways: by way of **reason**, and by way of **testimony** or **authority**. He could focus his reflections on this subject to penetrate its secret through his reasoning, and he could learn of this **Fact** from **God** himself who had formed him, to say nothing of the **Angels** who had been the witnesses of his **Creation**.

It appears that **Adam**, whose **Tradition** **Moses** has preserved, assumes that he had learned from **God** himself the manner in which he had been formed. **God** speaks to **Adam** and sanctifies the **seventh day**; that is to say, he obliges **Adam** to preserve the memory of his **Creation**; he binds him by a **Law** to think fifty-two times a year <note>The author refers here to the weekly Sabbath, calculating 52 weeks in a year as a recurring reminder of the six days of creation.</note> about the **Fact of Creation**, and to offer him continual thanks for the being he had received.

# GENESIS #

examining them by the lights of his reason.

The truth of a narrative consisting in the conformity of the Narrative with the Fact which is narrated, it clearly appears that Adam was able to know with certainty the truth of God’s Narrative, by examining the Facts in question.

One can distinguish several propositions in the narrative that God then gave him about the manner of his Creation: One, that his Body had been taken from the earth.

The other, that his **Soul** had not been formed before his **Body**.

The third, that the **Soul** had been joined to the **Body** to make but one and the same **Man**.

The fourth, that he had been created at a perfect age. <note>perfect age: a theological concept suggesting Adam was created as a fully developed adult, possessing full use of his faculties from the first moment.</note>

The fifth, that his **Wife** was formed from a rib that **God** took from his side, while he was in an ecstasy. <note>original: "extase"; in the context of 17th-century biblical commentary, this refers to the "deep sleep" (Hebrew: *tardemah*) mentioned in Genesis 2:21.</note>

These are truths that I propose as examples, because there are none among them of which Adam was the **Witness**.

Now I say that there is none among them of which he could not have assured himself by consulting the lights of his reason, & of which he could not have been convinced by the same means that made him know the existence of his **Soul** & that of his **Body**: The examination of these **Facts** could therefore serve, & must have served to confirm **God’s** narrative on the particulars of **Creation** of which **Adam** could not be the **Witness**.

# REFLECTIONS ON #

That his **Body** had been taken from the **Earth**, Adam could judge by the nature of the nourishment that sustained him. What else is food but earth digested and disguised, if one may express it so? <note>The author is using a common early modern philosophical argument: since humans eat plants and animals that grow from the soil, the human body must essentially be composed of the same terrestrial elements.</note> Certainly, as he found himself all at once in a perfect state; it was natural to judge that his **Body** had been produced all at once from the **Earth**, and not, as he saw happen thereafter, in the manner of the bodies of his **Children**, which received their proper size little by little.

He could also recognize just as easily that his **Soul** had not been formed before his **Body**: The **Soul** is a being that knows its own operations and thoughts. He had only to make this reflection: My **Soul** had neither knowledge nor thought before being united with my **Body**; my **Soul**, therefore, did not exist before my **Body**. It does not require a great effort of mind to draw a conclusion that is so natural. <note>This reflects a "tabula rasa" or Aristotelian view that the soul does not possess innate memories of a pre-existence, but begins its conscious operations upon union with the physical form.</note>

Could he be ignorant of the union of his **Soul** with his **Body**? Was it not easy to be certain of it by the prompt obedience that his **Body** rendered to his **Soul**? He received the idea of a tree just as well as the idea of his foot or his arm: "From where does it come," he could say to himself, "that I command my foot, that I move my arm, and that the same thing does not happen regarding a stone or a tree, although I have received the idea of them just as well as that of my foot or

### CHAP. IX. ###

->*That Adam was confirmed by his experience in the conviction he held regarding the Fact of Creation.*<-

When I suppose that Adam was able to recognize the truth of God's account by making these reflections which are natural, I do not see that anything strange can be found in this supposition, unless perhaps that I make Adam out to be a highly speculative Man. <note>In this context, a "speculative man" (French: *Homme spéculatif*) refers to someone who engages in deep philosophical or logical reasoning rather than just relying on immediate sensory experience.</note>

But if one is at all fair, one must consider these points, which justify that I attribute nothing to him that cannot justly belong to him.

First, Adam finding himself at a mature age <term>mature age: *âge parfait*, the theological concept that Adam was created as a fully developed adult with his intellectual faculties already at their peak</term>, he was not at all filled with the prejudices that the ignorance and weakness of

<meta>catchword: childhood</meta>

<vocab>Adam, God, Creation, reflection, Speculative Man, mature age</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that Adam's immediate intellectual maturity allowed him to verify God's account of creation through logical reflection on himself and nature. He asserts that Adam was a "speculative man" whose reason was unclouded by the typical biases developed during human childhood.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Creation, Reason vs. Revelation, Speculative Philosophy, Mature Age, Prejudices, Human Nature</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Adam's intellectual state was uniquely pure. Having been created as an adult, he lacked the childhood prejudices and social superstitions that usually cloud human judgment, allowing him to evaluate God’s account of creation with objective reason.</meta>

# GENESIS #

childhood gives rise to: His mind was not at all spoiled by popular beliefs <note>original: "créances populaires." This refers to common superstitions or folk traditions that the author believes hinder clear, logical thinking.</note>, which so greatly hinder the discovery of truth. He had not yet received any bad impressions; and when he was credulous enough to give way to temptation, his experience became a very powerful means to correct his reason and to pull him out of his wandering.

Furthermore, it is posited that the testimony of God, who explained to him in detail the manner in which He had created him, preceded Adam’s own reflections and served to bring them about. Even if a peasant is not capable of discovering for himself how a thing happened, at the very least it is certain that his common sense alone is enough to recognize that the thing must have happened as it was recounted to him; he knows how to consult the rules of probability well enough to make up his mind.

Not only was Adam formed at a mature age; not only did he have the testimony of God, who instructed him in the details of things: Not only during a life of several centuries was he able to apply himself to considering the probability of God’s account, to examining its foundations and certainty—in a word, to judging whether he should reject this account as mythical <note>original: "fabuleux," meaning legendary or belonging to the realm of fables rather than history.</note> or acquiesce to it as an incontestable truth—but here is something more: Adam was able to reasonably assure himself of the manner in which things he had not seen were made, by

The manner in which his Body had been produced from the Earth was, I admit, a difficult thing to understand; but the production of a Child similar to himself through the way of **generation** <term>The biological process of procreation or reproduction.</term> which he could experience, must have established in his Mind the belief that he owed to God; the way of generation being perhaps as much as, or more difficult to understand than the manner in which he had been created.

One cannot fail to recognize that Adam must have made, and that he truly did make, the reflections that I attribute to him, if one considers that God gave him a Law proportioned to the state in which he had been formed: For this Law assumes that Adam recognized himself as the Subject of God; that he regarded God as his benefactor; that he expected rewards from Him, and that he feared being punished by Him.

They were also able, in the space of so long a life as that which they enjoyed <note>This refers to the biblical tradition of the "Patriarchs" who lived for several centuries, allowing ample time for the transmission and verification of oral history.</note>, to examine the solidity of the reasons that persuaded Adam and his Wife of the truth of the Fact of their Creation.

### CHAP. XI.

->*That the Children of Adam were indeed convinced of the truth of the Fact of the Creation and the Promise of the Messiah.*<-

I think it appears clearly enough that the first descendants of Adam were able to assure themselves of the manner in which their parents had been formed, and how they themselves had received life. They were able to know with the same certainty the sin that Adam had committed, and the <term>Promise: known as the Protevangelium (Genesis 3:15), the first prophecy of a savior</term> that God had made to him, that someone would be born of his Posterity who would destroy the Enemy of Man.

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XI, transitioning from the theoretical possibility of the descendants of Adam knowing the truth to the practical evidence found in their actions and the transmission of their beliefs.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
# GENESIS.

We must therefore see if they were indeed assured of the truth of these **Facts**: this is something that can be easily clarified if we consider two things.

The first is that, since those **Facts** were the primary things that should have occupied their meditation, because of their singular importance, they did in fact act in recognition that those **Facts** were true.

The other thing is that as they had been instructed in them by their Fathers, they subsequently instructed their Posterity, to whom they transmitted the belief in these **Facts** as indubitable.

I say that they acted as being assured of the truth of these **Facts**, which appears in their conduct, not only when they performed good actions, but also when they fell into crimes; and it seems to me that this suffices to justify how profound was the impression that the Facts of the <term>Creation: the divine act of bringing the world into existence</term> and the <term>Promise: the "Protevangelium" or first gospel (Genesis 3:15), promising a future savior</term> had made upon their hearts.

**Sacrifices** are acts of **Religion**: the custom that the descendants of Adam had of offering **Sacrifices** to God thus marks their piety; and the **Religion** which animated them was visibly the fruit of the persuasion they held regarding the truth of the **Fact of the Creation** and that of the **Promise**.

The crime of Cain, who killed his Brother,

> *The Seed of the Woman shall crush the head of the Serpent*,
being expressed in very general terms, could apply either to the first of Eve’s male children, or to her posterity in general, or to one of those who would be born from her by being born from her descendants.

It is natural to move from one meaning to another when searching for the true sense of these types of general propositions, when one cannot doubt their truth. Now, it appears from the very name that Eve gave to Cain, that she took the words of this Oracle in the first sense. She considered Cain as the Son whom God had promised her:

A similar reflection should be made concerning the name that Eve gave to Seth, and the Jews lead us to this in their ancient Commentary on Genesis: She called, they say, his name Seth. For, she said, God has given me **another Posterity**. Rabbi Tanchuma <note>A famous 4th-century Palestinian rabbi</note>, following the thought of Rabbi Samuel, says that she was referring to that Posterity which was to come from another place, or from a foreign place: And who is this posterity? he asks; it is the King Messiah.

I do not claim that the reflection they made on the term **another Posterity** is entirely solid: they aimed rather to amuse themselves with a kind of wordplay on the word *another*, which is sometimes taken to mean "foreign," rather than saying something strictly accurate. But at the very least, one cannot deny that Eve, having considered her son Seth as the one whom God had given her in place of Abel, must have instructed him in this hope; and it is precisely this that not only helped form Seth and his Posterity in piety and the spirit of Religion, but which particularly moved them to separate themselves from the race of Cain, as from a race that God had stripped of the right to fulfill this first Oracle, to which it naturally laid claim.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the early history of mankind following the fall of Adam and the murder of Abel, specifically focusing on how the "Promise" of a future redeemer shaped the religious life and expectations of Adam's descendants.</meta>

->### REFLECTIONS ON<-

The children of Adam lived in the practice of religious worship. It is known that this worship presupposes the truth of the facts of Creation and of the Promise <note>The "Promise" refers to the "protoevangelium" in Genesis 3:15, where God promised a "seed" to the woman who would defeat the serpent</note>, and that all religious acts are intended either to set this truth before our eyes every seventh day <note>A reference to the Sabbath as a day of remembrance</note>, to explain its wonders, or to express to God the gratitude we owe Him for it.

However, one might even say that the example of Lamech's polygamy <note>Lamech, a descendant of Cain, was the first man in the biblical narrative to take two wives, representing a shift in the social and moral order</note> serves in some respect to demonstrate the strength of this conviction.

Since Cain had been rejected by God and punished with an exemplary chastisement after he put his brother Abel to death, it is visible that the Promise could no longer be understood in its primary sense <note>The "primary sense" being the expectation that Adam's immediate firstborn would be the promised redeemer</note>. Consequently, one could not apply the Oracle <note>original: "Oracle"; referring to the divine prophecy regarding the coming "seed"</note> concerning the promised Posterity to Adam's first son, nor to his first descendants. Since the truth of this Oracle could not be denied, it was only natural to give it another meaning and to seek its fulfillment in a more distant or more numerous Posterity.

Indeed, we see that what I am saying actually occurs: the exemplary punishment that God inflicted on Cain during the first seven generations—following the terms stating that he would be punished sevenfold—made his descendants understand that, because of the crime he had committed, God had justly stripped him of the right he would have had to the fulfillment of the Promise. at the same time they understood that this right, which belonged to the firstborn rather than the younger sons, must have returned to his offspring after the seventh generation. Indeed, Lamech <note>original: "Lemech"</note> fell into polygamy as if he intended to claim the fulfillment of the Promise <note>The "Promise" refers to the "protoevangelium" in Genesis 3:15, where God promised a "seed" to the woman who would defeat the serpent</note> by striving to multiply his descendants. I admit that he imitated the crimes of Cain and thus may have been steeped in his false maxims; but whatever the corruption of that time, as he was raised with his father’s hope and in contact with the family of Seth—who, several centuries earlier, had established public assemblies for acts of religion—it is unlikely that he did not share the same expectations.

Lamech's polygamy can therefore be rightly viewed as an effect of the prejudices he held regarding the meaning of the first Oracle <note>original: "Oracle"; referring to the divine prophecy regarding the coming "seed"</note>. He chose the means that seemed most suitable to share in the fulfillment of the Promise, which concerned the birth of an offspring. Yet, ultimately, this disorder into which he fell when he married two wives at the same time is authentic proof of his conviction regarding both the Promise:

->*The woman’s offspring shall crush the serpent’s head*,<-

as well as regarding Creation.

It seems that, according to these principles, it is natural to explain, on one hand, the acts of violence that were committed in that ancient world;

The children of **Adam** lived in the practice of religious worship. It is known that this worship presupposes the truth of the events of **Creation** and the **Promise** <note>The "Promise" refers to the protoevangelium (Genesis 3:15), the first prophecy of a savior who would be born of a woman.</note>, and that all religious acts are employed either to bring this truth back before one's eyes every seventh day <note>A reference to the Sabbath, established in the Creation narrative as a day of rest and remembrance.</note>, or to explain its wonders, or to show God the gratitude we owe Him for them.

But one can even say that the example of the **polygamy** of **Lamech** <note>original: "Lemech"</note> serves in some respect to show the strength of this conviction.

Since **Cain** had been rejected by God and punished with an exemplary penalty after he put his brother **Abel** to death, it is clear that one could no longer take the **Promise** in its primary sense; thus, one could not apply the **Oracle** <note>original: "Oracle"; meaning a divine prophecy.</note> concerning the promised **Descendant** to Adam's first son, nor to his first **Posterity**. Since one could not fail to recognize its truth, it was therefore natural to give it another meaning and to seek its fulfillment in a more distant or more numerous lineage.

Indeed, we see that what I am saying actually happened: the exemplary punishment that God inflicted on **Cain** during the first seven generations—according to the terms stating that he would be punished sevenfold <note>original: "puni par sept fois"; referencing Genesis 4:15 and 4:24.</note>—made his descendants understand that God had justly taken from him, because of the crime he had committed, the right he would have had to the execution of the **Promise**. Noah could not call into doubt either of these two Facts <note>The "Facts" referred to here are the Creation of the world and the Promise of the Messiah, which the author argues were preserved through oral tradition.</note>.

Noah was six hundred years old when the **Flood** arrived: He had therefore conversed with **Lamech**, his Father, who had seen **Adam** and his Children, being fifty-six years old when **Adam** died. He had conversed with **Methuselah**, his Grandfather, who died in the year of the **Flood**, and who—being three hundred and forty-three years old when **Adam** died—had instructed **Noah** during a long series of years, just as he himself had been instructed by **Adam** for several Centuries.

Since **Methuselah** had lived for a long time with **Seth** (who died in the year of the World 1042 <term>Year of the World: Often referred to by the Latin term *Anno Mundi*, this is a calendar era based on the biblical account of the creation of the world.</term>), it appears that **Noah**, who was born in the year of the World 1056, saw not only **Lamech** and **Methuselah**, but also several of their Ancestors, whose reasoning he heard, whose **Tradition** he examined, and whose devotions he imitated.

What is more, **Noah** saw that none of the Men knew of any other origin for his **Genealogy** than that which began with **Adam**. All those who were of his age could convince him of this. Each of them had as exact a knowledge of his Ancestors as he could possibly have.

This was easy to know with certainty for two reasons. One is the long life enjoyed by the Men of those **Centuries**. The other is the brevity of time between the **Flood** and the **Creation**.

He lived 118 years with **Selah**, son of Arphaxad, Selah having died in the year of the World 2126. He lived 179 years with **Eber**, son of Selah, Eber having died in the year of the World 2187.

**Isaac** being born in the year of the World 2108, he was able to see **Shem**, **Selah**, and **Eber**, who had conversed for hundreds of years with **Noah** and with his other **Children**. He was able to converse with these **Patriarchs**, or at least with those who, being of their age and speaking of **Noah** as a man of only yesterday, recounted—on the faith of Noah and on that of his children—the **Creation of Adam**, his **fall**, the **Promise of the Holy Posterity** <note>The "Promise" refers to the first prophecy of a Savior (Genesis 3:15) who would redeem humanity after the fall.</note>, the death of **Abel**, the **Miracle of Paradise**, the **Preaching of Noah**, the **Flood**, and so on. Furthermore, following the **Law of God** observed by their **Ancestors** before the Flood, they gathered fifty-two times a year <note>This likely refers to a weekly Sabbath or assembly for worship and oral instruction that the author believes existed before the formal Mosaic Law.</note> to celebrate the memory of these wonders and to teach them to their descendants.

One may note here three things that served to preserve the distinct knowledge of the **Facts** of which we speak: The first is that the **Ark** still remained in the eyes of the World, as a certain monument to the truth

### CHAP. XV.

->*That one sees the conviction of these same truths within the Family of Abraham, and among his Descendants down to Jacob.*<-

It is no less easy to understand how the distinct knowledge of the **Creation** and the **Promise of the Messiah** was able to reach **Jacob** and his descendants in its entirety: this is what I wish to explain briefly. I do not merely note here that the **Religion** practiced by **Abraham** and by his **Descendants** constantly presupposes the **Facts of the Creation** and of the **Promise** as being distinctly known; one may observe something more specific.

It cannot be denied that since **Lot** followed **Terah** <note>Tharé in the original; the father of Abraham.</note> and **Abraham** when **God** called **Abraham** from **Chaldea**, **Lot** believed by that very fact that this **Heavenly Calling** separated him from the rest of the **Posterity of Shem** <note>Sem in the original; one of the three sons of Noah from whom Abraham descended.</note>, and gave him, as well as **Abraham**, the right to claim the privilege of fulfilling the **Promise of the Messiah**, or of seeing it fulfilled in his own **Posterity**. curse pronounced against **Ham** <term>Ham: One of the sons of Noah; his descendants (the Canaanites) were traditionally viewed in this era as being excluded from the divine blessings promised to the lineage of Shem.</term>, having no part in the most precious of **Blessings**, they believed they had no right to marry into that line after the separation that **God** had made between their Father and that Family. On the other hand, they believed **God** would exempt them from the irregularity of their action, given that they were reduced to such an extreme necessity.

There are three circumstances that support the reflection I am making on the **incest** they committed:

The first is that they are represented to us as having maintained a very honorable conduct in the midst of the impurities of **Sodom**, and indeed, they did not bring themselves to commit this **incest** until after the death of their Mother.

The second is that they together planned an action that naturally creates conflict between even the most united persons when one is governed by a spirit of debauchery. Furthermore, the Scripture does not say that they continued in this **incest**.

The third is that, far from being ashamed of such a criminal act or hiding the knowledge of it from their Posterity, they gave the Children born of their **incest** names that perpetuated the memory of their deed. One named her son **Moab**, which is to say, *born of my Father*; and the other named hers **Ben-Ammi**, a name that carries nearly the same idea <note>original: "Benammi"; in Hebrew, this name means "Son of my people" or "Son of my kin," highlighting the internal family connection rather than a marriage to outsiders.</note> in the minds of those who hear it.

### CHAP. XVII.

->*That the Tradition, which shows that the conviction the Ancients held before Moses regarding the truth of the Facts of the Creation of the World and the Promise of the Messiah, cannot be suspect.*<-

I believe I have sufficiently justified that the Facts of the Creation and the Promise, and those which depended upon them, could be certainly known by Adam and by his descendants, and subsequently transmitted to posterity until the time of Moses.

<meta>catchword: has himself</meta> <note>original: "s'est"</note>

From Adam to Noah, there is only one man—namely, Methuselah—who joins their hands. <note>The author uses the phrase "donne la main" (gives the hand) to illustrate how Methuselah acts as a physical and temporal bridge between the first man and the survivor of the Flood.</note>

From Noah to Abraham, there is only one man—namely, Shem—who saw them both for quite a long time.

From Abraham to Joseph, there is only one man—namely, Isaac, the grandfather of Joseph.

From Joseph to Moses, there is only one man—namely, Amram, who was able to see Joseph for quite a long time.

These markers of time, which Moses so carefully recorded, clearly show that the facts of the **Creation** and of the **Promise of the Messiah** could be distinctly known. For, assuming this chain of descendants, it would not have been easy to make people believe a falsehood; the reason is that each person living in the time of Moses could trace his genealogy back to Adam by a path as short as that of Moses. Anyone who had put forward some fact contrary to public notoriety would have been treated as ridiculous. It was equally impossible to be ignorant of the truth of such important facts as those of the Creation and the Promise of the Messiah.

I also believe I have clearly shown that several actions, quite strange in nature, the memory of which Moses preserved and which the **Atheists** regard as full of absurdity, were founded upon a firm conviction of the truth of these facts, as throughout a long succession of centuries each of these Ancients

He asserts that this brevity made it impossible for the historical record to be corrupted or for false claims to be accepted as truth.</summary>

One cannot reasonably object that all of this is founded only on the uncertain authority of tradition. For, even if we suppose that tradition regarding facts of another nature would not be a solid enough foundation to establish belief, this specific tradition has very singular characteristics which demonstrate its authority.

First, it involves only a small number of people from Adam until Moses, who finally committed this tradition to writing.

Secondly, it assumes that those who preserved this tradition possessed a very long life, usually spanning many centuries. <note>The author is referring to the patriarchal lifespans recorded in Genesis (such as Methuselah's 969 years), which he argues minimized the "telephone game" effect of distorted information.</note>

Thirdly, it concerns facts that everyone desires to learn about, and which everyone has a right to reflect upon, having a personal interest in them because these facts are the principle of one's actions and the rule of conduct, whether civil or religious.

Fourthly, it assumes real physical signs that served to preserve the memory, such as the pains of childbirth, <note>A reference to the "curse" in Genesis 3:16, seen here as a constant reminder of the Fall.</note> the existence of Paradise before the Flood, and the continued existence of the Ark after the Flood. <note>In the author's time, it was widely believed that the physical remains of Noah's Ark could still be found on Mount Ararat.</note>

Fifthly, it assumes a public service, the acts of which are repeated fifty-two times a year to preserve the memory for posterity. <note>This refers to the weekly Sabbath, which served as a recurring communal "history lesson."</note>

Sixthly, it has been preserved in pas-

### C H A P. XVIII.

*Clarification on the manner in which Moses wrote, where it is shown that in writing the Book of Genesis he wrote only of things generally known.*

IT IS a truth that I first assumed, for which I have since provided proofs, and the means of which I have explained in the course of these remarks. But it is important to establish it here even more solidly, because it often happens that those who do not pay attention to the consequences that can be drawn from the opposing view, unintentionally undermine it <note>The "opposing view" refers to the idea that Moses knew nothing of these events and received them only through direct, supernatural dictation.</note>; under the pretext of upholding the divine Authority of the Book of Genesis: This gives cause for Atheists to view the Book of Genesis in the same way that Scholars view most Legends. <note>The author suggests that if Genesis is treated as a purely mystical text with no historical witnesses, skeptics will dismiss it as mere mythology or "legends" rather than historical fact.</note>

The Prophetic Spirit acts in two ways: one is by way of revelation, regarding things of which a Prophet has no knowledge. This is how St. John the Evangelist predicted the things to come that we read in the Book of Revelation. <note>Original: "Apocalipse." In the 17th century, "Apocalypse" was the standard name for the Book of Revelation.</note> All these events being hidden in the shadows of the future, it is impossible for him to have spoken of them if the Spirit had not revealed them to him immediately.

<vocab>Moses, Book of Genesis, Prophetic Spirit, St. John the Evangelist, Revelation, Atheists, Legends</vocab>

<meta>catchword: The other</meta>

<summary>The author explains that Moses wrote Genesis using well-known historical traditions rather than exclusive divine revelation. He argues this approach strengthens the book's authority against skeptics who might otherwise dismiss it as a fictional legend.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Genesis, Prophetic Spirit, Revelation, St. John the Evangelist, historiography, atheism, oral tradition</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the distinction between two types of prophetic inspiration. While the previous page defined "revelation" (divine knowledge of the unknown future), this page defines "direction" (divine guidance in recording known history). The author argues that Moses wrote Genesis using the latter, relying on reliable oral tradition rather than mystical visions.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>REFLECTIONS ON</header>

The other way is by path of direction <note>The author uses "direction" to mean a form of divine supervision where God ensures the accuracy of a human writer's historical record, rather than telling them something they couldn't possibly know.</note>, regarding things that are known to the Prophet, either because he was himself a witness to them, or because he learned them from those who were witnesses. This guidance of the Spirit consists in leading the Prophet in such a way that he writes the matters in question exactly as they were spoken, or just as they occurred. This is how the Evangelists St. Matthew and St. John wrote the summary of the Sermons of Jesus Christ that they had heard, and the Miracles they had seen; and how St. Luke and St. Mark wrote the things they learned from those who had been eyewitnesses, as St. Luke specifically explains. <note>Referring to Luke 1:1–4, where the author states he investigated accounts from eyewitnesses.</note>

I maintain that in writing the Book of Genesis, Moses possessed the second kind of Prophetic Influence, and not the first. Although, when arguing against Atheists to convince them by factual evidence, one could even consider him merely as an Historian who, without any other particular direction, would have written his Book and preserved the memory of ancient events that were generally known. <note>The author is walking a fine line: he believes Moses was divinely guided, but he argues that even a skeptic should respect Genesis as a reliable history book based on the strong oral traditions of the time.</note>

Whatever position theologians take on this matter when acting against Atheists, it is very easy for me to establish my claim; first, because it is not necessary to suppose an entire revelation where Tradition, being sufficiently distinct, clearly suffices...

<meta>catchword: ...ly (from the French 'manifestement')</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Moses wrote Genesis based on reliable historical tradition under divine "direction" rather than through direct revelation of unknown facts. ## CHAPTER XIX.

->*Response to the objection that may be drawn from the history of the **Egyptians** and the **Chaldeans** regarding the antiquity of the World.*<-

What I have just presented is sufficient to establish that **Moses** wrote nothing in the **Book of Genesis** except for things generally known by everyone. I know of nothing that can be opposed to this with any plausibility, except for what one reads concerning the history of **Egypt** in the most ancient Authors, to which must be added what those same ancient Authors tell us concerning the **Chaldeans** <note>An ancient people of Mesopotamia (modern-day Iraq) known for their early advancements in astronomy and recorded history.</note>. The history of **China** also provides an objection that seems quite plausible against what **Moses** tells us in the **Book of Genesis**. It is therefore necessary to examine both with attention, so as to leave no difficulty remaining on such an important Subject.

Here is what the **Atheists** <note>In the 17th century, "Atheist" was a broad term often applied to anyone whose historical or scientific views appeared to contradict the literal timeline of the Bible.</note> can say against the

->*Response to the objection that might be drawn from the History of China against what Moses says concerning the novelty of the World.*<-

## CHAP. XXI.

->Where one responds to the last objection of the Atheists.<-

**A**s I aim to leave no difficulty on such an important matter in the minds of those who read these Remarks, I believe I am obliged to anticipate and clearly resolve the only remaining objection that seems to me to have some appearance of weight, after all that I have said above.

It will perhaps be said that while it is admitted that Moses is the Author of the Book of Genesis, and that it could not have been forged under his name in following centuries, this does not mean that one can be convinced of the truth of the events he described. The reason is that Moses forced the People of Israel to receive the belief in the facts he recites, and that he prevented anyone from calling them into doubt when he established Laws in the Republic <term>Republic: Used here in the classical sense of a "commonwealth" or a structured political state.</term> of Israel, by virtue of which those who dared to contest the truth of his Books and the narratives he had inserted therein were punished by death. This is, as far as I can conceive, the limit to which the spirit of stubborn-

Scarcely forty years passed between Joshua's Conquest of the Land of Canaan and the servitude to which the Israelites were reduced under neighboring Peoples. Was this not the time to recognize the fabrication <note>original: "suposition," referring to the idea that Moses might have "supposed" or invented his history.</note> that Moses had made? Was the comparison they could make between the Tradition, as Moses had set it down in writing, and the traditions found among the Peoples who had subjugated them, not a natural and easy means to disabuse themselves?

## CHAP. XXII.

->Consequences of what has been established in these<-
->Reflections on the Book of Genesis.<-

**THESE** are the reflections that I believe can be made quite naturally on the Book of Genesis, and I believe they are sufficient to establish very solidly the truth of the Facts of the Creation of the World, and of the Promise of the Messiah <note>The "Promise of the Messiah" refers to the theological interpretation of Genesis 3:15, often called the *Protevangelium*, which Christians view as the first prophecy of a savior.</note>, which are the foundations of the Christian Religion.

I maintain that Moses, this famous Hebrew who was destined to be the heir of Pharaoh’s daughter <note>According to the biblical book of Exodus, Moses was rescued from the Nile and adopted by the daughter of the Egyptian King (Pharaoh).</note>, is the author of the Book of Genesis.

I maintain that, this being granted, he could not, in the character he gave to his history, have recounted the important facts of which he speaks otherwise than as they had actually happened.

I maintain that although he was not a witness to the Creation, he recounted the manner of it, based on an authentic tradition and on a public testimony which cannot be called into doubt, since at that time it was known not only by the Moabites, and the Ammonites, the Ishmaelites, and the Edomites <note>These were neighboring nations to the Israelites who, according to the Bible, shared common ancestors (Lot, Ishmael, and Esau) and thus would have possessed similar oral traditions regarding their origins.</note>, among whom he had traveled for forty years, and who were

<summary>In this concluding chapter, the author asserts that Moses is the definitive author of Genesis and argues that the historical accounts therein, including Creation, were corroborated by the shared traditions of neighboring nations.</summary>

...of the Creation cannot be more invincibly proven than by the Book of Genesis, Moses having preserved for us the public Tradition of the Centuries <note>In this context, "centuries" refers to the entire span of human history from the beginning of the world until the time of Moses.</note> that had preceded his own, and having done nothing but put into writing what was then distinctly known by all the Earth, in a time when everyone easily traced the beginning of his Genealogy back to Adam <note>The author assumes a "short chronology" common in the 17th and 18th centuries, where only a few thousand years—and a relatively small number of generations—were thought to separate the first humans from the era of Moses.</note>.

I maintain, finally, that as one cannot reasonably contest the Fact of the Creation, nor the other Facts that depend on it, without rendering useless all the proofs of Fact that I have employed on this matter, there is nothing so just as to receive that Fact as an incontestable Fact.

It is a Fact reported by the oldest Historian of the World. It is a Fact sustained by the oldest Tradition of the World. It is a Fact received by the oldest People of the World. Finally, it is a Fact of which this People and its Ancestors before Moses have constantly celebrated the memory by virtue of a public Law, fifty-two times each year, going back seven days by seven days, until the day of Creation <note>The "public Law" and the practice of celebrating "fifty-two times each year" refers to the Sabbath, the weekly day of rest which the author views as a living monument or historical record of the seven-day Creation account.</note>.

# PREFACE.

When I first committed to writing my *Reflections on the Book of Genesis*, my sole intention was to prove the truth of the *Fact of the Creation of a first Man*, and to establish by factual evidence that this Truth—no more than the *Fact of the Promise of the Messiah* that *God* made to Man after the sin—cannot be reasonably disputed.

I had afterwards resolved to establish the truth of the *Resurrection of our Lord Jesus Christ* by a similar factual proof, by showing that the *Apostles* were *Eyewitnesses* to this *Event*, and that they instituted a solemn day in each *Week* to commemorate it every seven days: And this seemed to me sufficient to establish the truth of the *Christian Religion*. Indeed, just as the *Jews* could trace back, by means of the *Sabbath* <term>Sabbath: The Jewish day of rest, traditionally commemorating God's rest after the six days of Creation</term>, to the day of *Creation*, *Christians* can likewise trace back, by means of *Sunday* to the day of the *Resur-*

<sig>L 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: rection</meta>

<summary>In this opening of the preface, the author outlines his objective to prove the historical reality of Creation and the Resurrection using the continuity of weekly religious observances as evidence. He argues that the Sabbath and Sunday serve as unbroken links to the foundational events of the Judeo-Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Genesis, Messiah, Resurrection of Christ, Apostles, Christian Religion, Jews, Sabbath, Sunday, historical evidence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Preface. # PREFACE.

rection of our Lord <note>The text continues from the previous page's break: "Resur-rection".</note>: And considering the matter in itself, it is as ridiculous to suspect the slightest fabrication <note>original: "suposition," meaning a fraudulent invention or forgery</note> regarding the **Fact of the Resurrection of our Lord Jesus Christ**, as it is regarding the **Fact of the Creation of a first Man**.

Furthermore, it is natural to conclude that if this proof is found to be solid, nothing is more strongly demonstrated than the truth of the **Christian Religion**; if the truth of the **Resurrection of our Lord** is once solidly established, it leads one to accept immediately that he is truly the promised **Messiah** <term>Messiah: Derived from the Hebrew 'Mashiach' (Anointed One), referring to the savior promised in the Hebrew Bible and identified by Christians as Jesus.</term>; and one must necessarily embrace his Religion.

But it happened to me imperceptibly, while meditating on **Genesis**, that I went further into the **Fact of the Promise** than I had originally intended. Thus, instead of moving on now to the proof of the **Resurrection of Jesus Christ**, which shows in brief that he is the Messiah, I found myself compelled by the way I had approached the subject to continue these kinds of *Reflections on the Books of Holy Scripture*, to show in greater detail that **Jesus Christ** is the Messiah whom **God** promised to Man immediately after his sin.

This is what I hope to show clearly and in a manner that will reveal that **God** has exactly followed his original designs <note>original: "projets"</note> and the first ideas he gave to those ancient **Patriarchs** <term>Patriarchs: The early biblical figures such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, who are considered the fathers of the faith.</term>. I will therefore first explain, as far as I have been able to understand it, the method God maintained to ensure the Messiah would be recognized when he was to appear in the world.

# PREFACE.

Secondly, I will reflect on the ideas that he gave long before in his **Oracles** <term>Oracles: Here referring to divine utterances or prophecies found in the Old Testament through which God's will was revealed.</term>, to characterize his Person, his **Offices** <note>original: "Charges," referring to the theological roles of the Messiah as Prophet, Priest, and King.</note>, his Actions, his Sufferings, his Glory, etc. Finally, I will show that this design is very exactly accomplished in the **History of the Gospel**, as the Disciples of **Jesus Christ** have left it to us. As this plan, which **God** specifically formed to make the **Messiah** known with certainty, appears to me in the **Books of Moses** that follow **Genesis**, I believed I should pause particularly on the examination of these Books, to clarify various things that deserve careful reflection, especially when one comes to consider them jointly and from a single viewpoint.

The **Prophecies**, in their connection and their progress, serve to explain this design of **God** more distinctly; this is something I believe is very capable of convincing the Jews, and of confounding them. This will oblige me to consider the **Oracles** with attention, and to unite them together to increase their light.

After that, I am persuaded that a little application of the mind to the **Gospel** will suffice to make it clearly known that **Jesus Christ** is the **Messiah**, which I propose to establish as the conclusion of this entire **Treatise**.

As I dedicated the first part of these *Reflections* to the examination of the **Religion of the Patriarchs** <note>The "Religion of the Patriarchs" refers to the faith and practices of the era of the ancestors, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, prior to the formal laws given at Mount Sinai.</note>, up to the time of **Moses** who described it in the **Book of Genesis**; I intend this second Part for the examination of the **Religion of the Israelites**, for which **Moses** has drawn up the plan in the four following Books <note>These four books are Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note>.

# PREFACE.

It is of extreme importance for the execution of this design to prevent anyone from calling into doubt the truth of these four Books <note>The author refers to the four books of the Pentateuch following Genesis: Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy.</note>. Thus, I must begin by establishing that **Moses** is their true Author, and that these Books indeed possess internal characteristics of an incontestable truth. Next, it is natural to show that this **Moses** had before his eyes the great **Promise of the Liberator** <term>Liberator: A term for the Messiah, whom the author argues was the central focus of biblical history from the beginning.</term> who had been the object of hope for the first Men before him, and whom **God** had particularly promised to the **Patriarchs** <note>The Patriarchs: Specifically the biblical fathers Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, to whom God made specific covenants regarding their descendants and a future savior.</note> of his Nation. In the third place, I will show that if one examines the **Laws of Moses** with attention, one finds there a plan which, on one hand, is very consistent with the design that **God** had previously followed—as seen in the **Book of Genesis**—and on the other, is very worthy of the Wisdom of **God**, if one considers the **Prophets** who followed the time of **Moses**, and what he wished to explain to us of his **Intentions** <note>original: "Intentions," referring to God's ultimate plan for salvation as revealed through progressive revelation.</note> through them.

## CHAPTER I.

->*That one cannot doubt that Exodus and the three following Books are by Moses.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter "C" featuring floral and leaf scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>It is a truth that can be established through entirely solid proofs. I could observe that even the Pagans <note>original: "Payens," referring to the non-Jewish and non-Christian ancient authors, such as those of Greece and Rome.</note> have recognized **Moses** <note>original: "Moïse."</note>, not only as the most ancient **Historian** of the World, but also as the oldest of **Legislators** <term>Legislator: A law-giver; the author emphasizes that Moses's role as a creator of a legal system was recognized even by secular ancient history.</term>.
