<header>REFLECTIONS ON THE</header>

I could also observe that the Book of Genesis and those following are in a very close connection, both regarding the subject matter and the general design of their Author. In fact, most of the Laws and Events found in these last four Books have their origin and occasion in the Facts recited in the Book of Genesis. We see that the claim of the Israelites to the Land of Canaan is founded on the Promise that God had made to **Abraham** and his Children to give it to them in the fourth Generation. We see that the war of the Amalekites <note>original: "Hamalekites"</note> against the Children of Israel is but a consequence of the Alliance of Amalek with the Canaanites, which is why his Posterity was the first to oppose the establishment of the Israelites in the Country of Canaan. <margin>Numbers 16</margin> We see likewise the Seditions of the Israelites under the leadership of **Korah, Dathan, and Abiram** <note>original: "Coré, Dathan & d'Abiram"</note> against **Moses**, founded on the order of birth of the Children of Israel as it is found in Genesis, those who were the eldest being unwilling to yield. But we must go further.

I say first, then, that it appears these Books, which contain Miracles, Laws, and Prophecies, were placed not only in the hands of the Principals and Heads of Families of the People of Israel, but also that they were placed in the **Tabernacle** <term>Tabernacle: The portable tent-sanctuary used by the Israelites during the Exodus; the author argues its use as a central archive proves the public and official nature of Moses's writings.</term> with great solemnity as a public trust: **Moses** himself placed them there a little

### REFLECTIONS ON THE SIX. <note>The "Six" likely refers to the Hexateuch, the first six books of the Old Testament (Genesis through Joshua).</note>

<meta>catchword: remon-</meta>

in the thirty-first [chapter] of Deuteronomy, verses ten and eleven, regarding what was ordained for instructing Posterity <term>Posterity: Future generations of descendants.</term>, as seen in the thirteenth verse of the same Chapter. And regarding this, it is important to consider the conduct of God to prevent any forgery <note>original: "suposition" — in this context, it refers to the fraudulent substitution of false documents or claims of authorship.</note> occurring in this matter.

One sees up until the time of Moses, when the lives of men were much longer, a fairly consistent custom among the Patriarchs; it is that, being on their deathbeds, they placed before the eyes of their Posterity the most important truths and the capital Articles of Religion; as indeed, it is at the moment of death that teachings, appearing more sincere, acquire a new authority. In this regard, you have the examples of an Abraham, an Isaac, a Jacob, and a Joseph: and one should not doubt that this custom had descended from Adam down to these Patriarchs. This custom visibly served to perpetuate the memory of illustrious Facts: And it was a kind of very public Sermon, since it took place within large Families, who gathered then at the bed of their common Leader, in a circumstance that very strongly awakened their attention.

Here is a custom that was substituted for the previous one. It is that the most Illustrious Leaders of the People of Israel assembled this People shortly before their death, and addressed them with exhortations, censures, and [remonstrances...] <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the word "remonstrances."</note>

One sees in these Books an unadorned history <note>original: "naïve" — in this period, the word meant "natural," "sincere," or "unfiltered" rather than "gullible."</note> which precisely marks the circumstances of places, times, and persons; it often describes things that seem of little importance, and describes them with more exactitude than the greatest and most surprising events. One finds events recounted there that an Author living after Moses would have infallibly removed, in order to abolish the memory of certain actions that tended to dishonor powerful Families and entire Tribes.

One sees inserted there with care the History of the Songs <note>original: "Cantiques" — referring to poetic and musical records like the Song of the Sea in Exodus 15.</note> and other public Monuments, which served to preserve the memory of the extraordinary events that are recounted to us therein.

Three characteristics can be added to the preceding ones. The first is that these Books have been constantly cited by all those who followed the time of Moses, and one finds therein that which has been cited by subsequent Authors: This is the sure mark of the sincerity of the Works, as well as of their antiquity. To agree upon this characteristic, one need only trace back from the time when they were translated into Greek, about three hundred years before—

It is therefore impossible that in the belief which the entire Jewish people have always held regarding the divinity of these Books and their authority, the slightest change could have occurred in this regard.

Indeed, one sees with surprise that after the transport of most of the ten Tribes to Assyria, those who were sent from Assyria to inhabit the land that the Israelites occupied <note>These inhabitants were the ancestors of the Samaritans.</note> received this Law, and that their descendants preserve it today just as we find it in the hands of the Jews, even though this nation—enemies of the Jews—has undergone all the upheavals <note>original: "revolutions" — here meaning political and social changes over time.</note> to which a nation is subject during the span of twenty-four centuries.

The third characteristic is worthy of serious reflection, and shows that, despite the corruption that occurred in the state of the Israelites, these Books have always remained in the same form as we have them today. It appears from the History of Josiah, recorded in the twenty-third [chapter] of the second Book of Kings, verse 21, that after Moses’ original manuscript <term>Autograph of Moses|The physical scroll believed to have been written by Moses himself, rediscovered in the Temple during Josiah's reign.</term> was found, and after this Law had been read publicly, the Passover was celebrated according to what was written in the Book of this Covenant. Now, the institution of this feast is seen in the twelfth [chapter] of Exodus, which shows that the "Book of this Covenant" refers to the Books of Moses as we have them, and that Moses placed them in this same state within the Sanctuary.

# IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS.

It is important to reflect upon what is reported throughout this chapter of the Second Book of Kings <note>The author refers to 2 Kings 23, which details King Josiah's religious reforms.</note>, for it shows clearly that it was according to this Law that they began to reform all the abuses that had crept into the Religion and which the authority of the Kings had introduced from Solomon until Josiah. This chapter contains the history of Josiah, but in relation to the opposing conduct of his predecessors, and to the public monuments of their superstitions, which he solemnly abolished in order to obey the Laws of God contained in the Books of Moses.

Furthermore, it is certain that it was by no means easy to deceive posterity regarding these matters. First, the facts contained in the Books of Moses did not concern only the People of Israel, but also most of the neighboring peoples. Secondly, one can say that the memory of such important events was so vivid that nearly fifteen centuries after Moses, the names of the Magicians <note>original: "Mages" — referring to the Egyptian sorcerers Jannes and Jambres who opposed Moses.</note> mentioned in the eleventh verse of the seventh chapter of Exodus were <margin>2 Timothy 3</margin> still known, not only among the Jews—as seen by a passage from Saint Paul who identified them by name—but also among the Pagans, as is seen <margin>Natural History, Book 30, ch. 1</margin> by the testimony of Pliny and * Numenius <margin>* Cited in Eusebius, Preparation for the Gospel, Book 8, ch. 8</margin>.

This is the double advantage of the Books of Moses, which prevents one from being able to suspect in them the slightest forgery <note>original: "suposition" — in this context, it refers to the act of fraudulently substituting a false text for a genuine one.</note>.

But we see that Moses acted differently: His regulations are supported by the account of significant events; he gives almost no important law without explaining to the Jews what obliges them to obey it, and without compelling them by this means to reflect on the benefits they had received from God, or on the miracles <note>original: "merveilles" — literally "marvels" or "wonders," here referring specifically to the divine miracles performed during the Exodus.</note> they themselves had witnessed.

Furthermore, one sees Religion so bound up in Politics there, that the latter seems to subsist only by the grace of the former, contrary to the common practice of human Legislators, who seem to have less in view the establishment of Religion and religious Society than the survival and preservation of the State.

If a Jew wished to set before his eyes the Tradition of his Ancestors regarding the things that had preceded him, Moses has preserved it with such care, such marks of fidelity, and in such a clear manner, that it is his History alone in the World that can clarify the origin of things—the Fables, which make up what is oldest in the Tradition of the Pagans, being after all nothing but a corruption of the truths that Moses described Historically.

Finally, if the Jews wished to know what the state of their Republic was to be in the course of time, Moses explains it so particularly that they had cause to regard his Predictions—which he proposed, moreover, as coming immediately from God—not simply as Predictions, but as a History of future events, sketched in advance and traced before their fulfillment.

# REFLECTIONS ON THE
## circumstances regarding the person of Moses, which have elevated him far above ordinary Authors.

The first is that whereas Men take extreme care to hide their weaknesses and what is disadvantageous to their Family, it seems on the contrary that Moses made a point of writing things that could tarnish the memory of his Ancestors, and of preserving the memory of his own sins. He tarnishes the memory of Jacob <note>The biblical patriarch and father of the twelve tribes.</note> in that he had preferred Joseph, the son of his beloved Wife <note>Rachel.</note>, over Reuben his eldest, whom he deprives of the Birthright <term>Primogeniture</term> <note>The right of the firstborn to inherit the largest share and leadership. Moses later codifies laws that actually prevent a father from unfairly favoring the son of a "beloved" wife over a firstborn son from a "hated" one (Deuteronomy 21:15–17).</note> to give that right to Joseph; a practice that Moses forbids by an express Law. He tarnishes the memory of Levi, the Head of his Family, by reporting both his violence against the Shechemites <note>Referring to the massacre of the city of Shechem by Levi and Simeon (Genesis 34).</note> and the dispersion of his descendants among the other Tribes.

He tarnishes his own birth within his Laws by forbidding the Marriage of an Aunt with her Nephew, he who was born of Jochebed <note>In Exodus 6:20, Jochebed is described as the sister of Amram’s father, making her his aunt.</note> married to Amram her Nephew. He describes his own unbelief with extreme candidness, and it often seems that he only speaks of himself—when representing the Miracles that God performed through his hands—in order to display his own faults: This character strongly establishes the sincerity of an Author.

The second Reflection is that Moses did not strive to leave the Empire <note>In this context, "Empire" refers to supreme authority or sovereign leadership over the nation.</note> to his Family. He leaves the leadership of the People of Israel to Joshua, who was of the Tribe of Ephraim, and his

his Family is found buried among the group of Levites, who were of a rank below that of the Priests <note>The "Sacrificateurs" or Priests refers specifically to the descendants of Aaron, Moses's brother. Moses's own sons remained common Levites, who performed secondary duties in the Tabernacle but held no special religious or political status.</note>. The third Reflection is that Moses had no part in the Conquest of the Land of Canaan, for it was all done after his death; and this is what properly served to establish this Republic <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, scholars often referred to the biblical nation of Israel as a "Republic" to describe its unique system of governance under God's laws.</note> which was previously wandering in the Desert, and to give it a regular form. How could a plan as singular as that found in the Law of Moses be executed by someone other than the one who formed it, and be maintained through subsequent centuries? Joshua is the one who made this Conquest of the Country of Canaan; it is he who put the Jews in possession of this Country, and to whom they are indebted. Now, who does not see that after such a great change the People of Israel, being in a position to reject the Laws of Moses, would have done so infallibly, if their Divine authority had not been clearly known to them; if Joshua and those who followed him in Government had not held for Moses and all his Regulations a complete veneration?

One should note a rather surprising detail regarding the account of this Lawgiver's death: it is that his body was not found after his death. And indeed, we see that this People, who carried the bones of Joseph <note>The Patriarch Joseph, son of Jacob, whose bones were brought out of Egypt (Exodus 13:19).</note> and his brothers with such care to bury them in the tombs of their ancestors (for Joseph and his brothers were buried in Judea), have not shown the tomb of Moses their Lawgiver in any location?

Could *Moses* have been deceived regarding this matter? Or could *Moses* have deceived anyone about it? Was it not a well-enough known fact, the rigor with which the *Jews* were forced by public edicts during his childhood to throw their male children into the *Nile* River? The public works and the fortified cities they had built, *Pithom* and *Ramesses*—so named after the name of King *Ramesses Miamun* <note>Original: "Ramses Miamum." This likely refers to Ramesses II, whose throne name included "Usermaatre-setepenre" and whose personal name was "Ramesses Meriamun," meaning "Beloved of Amun."</note>—did they not still stand before the eyes of the whole world? And could the manner in which they were built have been an unknown fact?

His education could not have been unknown to him either, nor to any of the *Hebrews*, even if one does not add to *Moses’s* account everything that *Josephus, the Jewish Historian* <note>Referring to Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century author of "Antiquities of the Jews," who included extra-biblical traditions about Moses's life as a general in Ethiopia.</note>, relates to us regarding the person of *Moses*, his exploits at *Meroë*, his marriage to an *Ethiopian Princess*, etc.

Since the miracles of *Moses* were performed in the presence of the *Egyptians* and the *Jews*, it is evident that they establish the truth of the fact of his Divine Calling in the *Desert*, just as he described it. Now, one cannot dispute the truth of these miracles if one considers the following points.

First, it cannot be denied that the People of *Israel* left *Egypt* under the leadership of *Moses*. The *Law* which they still observe today states this in a hundred places, and it is a fact that has been and is still today constantly recognized. The most

<margin>Josephus, *Against Apion*, Book 1.</margin>
Ancient authors have written of it <note>Referring to the Exodus of the Israelites from Egypt</note> unanimously. We also have the testimony of Manetho <note>An Egyptian priest and historian (3rd century BC) whose works are often cited by Josephus to provide an Egyptian perspective on the Exodus.</note> on this subject, who recounts the time, the manner, and the principal circumstances. Trogus Pompeius, Justin, and Tacitus <note>Citing a lineage of Roman historians who discussed Jewish origins, often with a mix of factual and biased information.</note> support it after him, although Tacitus falsely reports without proof that the **Jews** were driven out of **Egypt** because of their leprosy: indeed, it is known that leprosy was a disease peculiar to the **Egyptians**, who gave it to the **Israelites**; it is far from the case that the **Egyptians** could have driven out the **Israelites** to deliver themselves from this ailment.

<margin>Justin, Book...</margin>
It is further known that the **Law of Moses**, which designates this illness as the “Egyptian Disease,” banished lepers from society; and the manner in which **David** pronounces imprecations against **Joab** regarding the murder of **Abner** <note>In 2 Samuel 3:29, David curses Joab’s lineage to always be afflicted with sores or leprosy as punishment for murder.</note> shows clearly enough that leprosy was considered by the **Jews** as an affliction sent by God. Finally, Trogus Pompeius himself noted that the **Magicians** <note>Original: "Mages." Referring to the court sorcerers of Pharaoh who attempted to match Moses's miracles.</note> caused **Moses** and the **Israelites** to be driven out because they themselves had been struck by a scab and an itch <note>Original: "galle & d’une gratelle." Likely referring to the plague of boils described in the Book of Exodus.</note> which they feared would spread throughout all **Egypt**.

<margin>Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 9, Chapter 8.</margin>
One must report here the testimony of Numenius, a Pythagorean Philosopher, who testifies that **Jannes** and **Jambres** <note>Names traditionally given to the Egyptian magicians who opposed Moses, mentioned in 2 Timothy 3:8 and various Jewish traditions.</note> were chosen by the **Egyptians** to oppose the miracles of **Moses**, whose prayers to God had reduced **Egypt** to extreme calamity at the time when the **Jews** were driven out of **Egypt**.

Secondly, if one maintains that the leprosy the **Jews** brought from **Egypt** is an indisputable mark of their stay there, one can also say that the departure of a great number of **Egyptians**—who so thoroughly aligned themselves with the interests of the **Hebrews** that they left in their company <note>This refers to the "mixed multitude" mentioned in Exodus 12:38, a group of non-Israelites who followed Moses out of Egypt.</note>—could have had no other motive than the many Miracles of **Moses** to which they had been witnesses. Furthermore, the descendants of these **Egyptians**, who were incorporated into the Body of the People of **Israel**, served as a collection of unimpeachable witnesses to the truth of the Miracles that bound their fathers to **Moses**, and which led them to follow the fortune of the People of **Israel**.

Thirdly, it is certain that the **Jews** could not have recounted any of the Miracles they claimed had occurred in the time of their fathers without exposing themselves to the mockery of the entire world, if these facts had not been recognized by the **Egyptians** themselves and by all their neighbors, to whom they were several times subjected <note>Referring to the period of the Judges, during which the Israelites were frequently conquered or ruled by neighboring nations like the Moabites, Midianites, and Philistines.</note> after the deaths of **Moses** and **Joshua**.

To all these reflections one must add that the departure of the **Hebrews** out of **Egypt**, with all the Miracles that **Moses** recounts, is a fact whose memory has been preserved from year to year, not only by the solemn Feast of **Passover** <note>Original: "Pâque." The central Jewish holiday commemorating the liberation from Egyptian slavery.</note>—a feast whose establishment is recorded for us by **Moses** in the book of **Exodus**, which the **Israelites** observed constantly until the time of their dispersion, and which they still imitate today throughout the world,

### Continuation of the Proofs of the truth of the Miracles reported by Moses.

ONE could be satisfied with these proofs of the truth of the **Miracles** that **Moses** recounts. But one can still add something further. There is nothing, seemingly, more ridiculous than what is reported concerning the arrival of the **Gibeonites** <note>Original: "Gabaonites." These were local inhabitants who, fearing the Israelite conquest, disguised themselves as weary travelers from a distant land to secure a peace treaty.</note> at the Camp of **Joshua**. Their gear and appearance suggested they wished to pass for people coming from a country very far from **Canaan**; the **Treaty** they obtained from **Joshua** by trickery is quite singular. Finally, is there anything so remarkable, on one hand, as their survival in the midst of the **People of Israel**, even though they were among the nations that **God** had ordered to be exterminated; and on the other, the role **Joshua** assigned them: to carry the wood and water necessary for the service of the **Tabernacle** <note>The portable sanctuary that preceded the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>? This caused them to bear the name of **Nethinim** <note>Original: "Nethiniens." This Hebrew term refers to "given ones" or "dedicated ones"—a class of temple servants.</note>, which always distinguished them from the **Israelites**.

But at its heart, there is nothing so worthy of **God** as the preservation of this **People** in the midst of **Israel**. The **Gibeonites** are the witnesses to the **Miracles** performed by **Moses** and by **Joshua**. These **Miracles** had compelled them to seek a **Covenant** <note>Original: "Alliance."</note> with the **Hebrews**, and to secure by deception a **Treaty** they did not believe they could obtain. Their survival in the midst of **Israel**, in a role that exposed them to the sight of the entire **Nation**, thus compelled the **Jews** to cherish the memory of those glorious events that every **Gibeonite** brought back before their eyes.

One can make a similar reflection on the survival of the Family of **Rahab** <note>A woman of Jericho who assisted the Israelite spies; her family was spared during the city's destruction and assimilated into the Hebrew people.</note> among the **Israelites** after the ruin of **Jericho**.

It is certain that the **Jews** have always maintained that since those first **Miracles** reported by **Moses**, a great number have been performed in their favor, whether during the **Conquest** they made of the **Land of Canaan**, or to preserve them there, or to restore them to its possession. One even finds some **Miracles** entirely similar to those first ones; for example, the death of one hundred and eighty-five thousand Men of the Army of **Sennacherib** <note>Referring to the miraculous defeat of the Assyrian army outside Jerusalem (2 Kings 19:35).</note>, the manner in which the shadow of the **Sun** returns on the sundial of **Ahaz** <note>A miracle performed for King Hezekiah where the shadow moved backward as a sign from God (2 Kings 20:11).</note>, the miraculous deliverance of their **Nation** by means of **Esther** which gave rise to the **Feast of Purim**, and various other miraculous events with which the **Books of the Jews** are filled.

But I wish at present to produce only three which preserved the memory of what was greatest in the wonders that **Moses** relates. I. the first is the continual **Miracle** of the **Urim** and the **Thummim** <note>Sacred objects or lots used by the High Priest to determine God's will.</note> of the **High Priest**; this **Miracle**, so frequent, confirmed the truth of **Moses'** Account concerning all the appearances of **God** to lead His **People**, and to give them His **Laws**.

each year preceding the seventh brought forth, so to speak, a triple harvest; <note>Referring to the biblical promise in Leviticus 25:21, where God pledges that the sixth year’s harvest would be sufficient to last through the Sabbatical year (the seventh) and until the harvest of the eighth year.</note> this continual **Miracle** occurring every seven years preserved the memory of the one God had performed in the **Desert**, by nourishing his **People** with **Manna** for forty years, and by doubling the measure of this **Manna** every sixth day. <note>According to Exodus 16, a double portion of Manna fell on Fridays so that the Israelites would not have to gather it on the Sabbath.</note>

Indeed, since this **Law** concerning the rest of the **Land** every seventh year was constantly observed, and since it would have been naturally rejected by this **People** if centuries of experience had not justified its **Divinity**; and as this **Miracle** was so well recognized that God, through his **Prophets**, when reproaching the **Jews** for the transgression of the **Sabbath**, threatened to withdraw the fertility of the sixth year, it is clear that one cannot contest the continual **Miracle** that this **Law** supposes and promises.

The third is the **Miracle** of a continual protection during the time of the three solemn **Festivals** when God obliged the **People** to go to the **Tabernacle** or to **Jerusalem**. <note>The three pilgrimage festivals: Passover, Shavuot (Pentecost), and Sukkot (Tabernacles).</note> This **Law**, regarding all **Jews** from the age of twenty and above, necessarily forced them to leave the **Frontiers** of the **Country** stripped of **Soldiers** and exposed to the attacks of **Enemies**; but it caused them to continually experience a protection similar to that which they read God had granted to their **Fathers**.

And certainly, was there ever a **Republic** founded upon a **Law** of this nature? Was the peril to which it exposed the **Country** not...

Furthermore, **God** so desired that the **Jews** be convinced of this miraculous help, that he punished by example and through a kind of **Miracle** an action of **David** which seems at first innocent enough for a **Prince**, but which, in essence, directly clashed with that confidence that the **People of Israel** ought to have had in the protection of **God**. **David** having had the **People** counted, <note>Referring to the census described in 2 Samuel 24 and 1 Chronicles 21, where David's decision to count his fighting men was viewed as a sin of pride and reliance on human strength rather than divine providence.</note> **God** sent a **Plague** which humbled the pride of **David**, and which taught him to place his trust in **God**, far more than in the multitude of the **People** who were subject to him.

## CHAP. V.

### That one cannot doubt the accounts Moses gives of the manner in which the Laws were given to him by God.

IT is no less easy to show that what **Moses** tells us about the manner in which the **Law** was given by **God** in the **Wilderness**, and what he reports concerning the different parts—**Moral**, **Political**, and **Ceremonial**—which compose it, is a constant and certain fact.

I will not pause here to consider the supreme equity and supreme perfection of the **Moral Law**, <note>Referring to the Ten Commandments and the ethical foundations of the Torah.</note> to show that it can have no author other than **God**. Nor do I wish to emphasize here the care that **God** took to regulate even the smallest **Ceremonies**, so as to leave nothing undecided in acts of **Religion**; nor the entirely **Divine** wisdom that appears in these **Ceremonial Laws**. It is nonetheless true that, in their opposition to the **Pagan** customs that prevailed at that time, most of them contain simple <note>original: "naïves" — in this 17th-century context, it means natural, vivid, or direct representations rather than "naive" in the modern sense.</note> images of our duties, or serve to make known future things that the **Gospel** has explained to us.

### IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS.

If one reads, for example, the eighteenth chapter of **Leviticus** <note>Leviticus is the third book of the Bible, largely concerned with the laws of holiness and the duties of the priests.</note>, one will find that **God** gives **Laws** there against **incestuous marriages**, which the **Canaanites** and the **Egyptians** undoubtedly contracted by relying on the example of the **Ancients** and their **Marriages** contracted within forbidden degrees. One will likewise find the prohibition of **Sacrifices** offered to **Molech** <note>Molech was a Canaanite deity; these rituals often involved the passing of children through fire.</note>, whose practice these **Peoples** very likely supported by the example of **Abraham** <note>This refers to the "Binding of Isaac" in Genesis 22, where Abraham's willingness to sacrifice his son was seen as the ultimate act of faith, though God ultimately forbade the act.</note>.

Now, who could imagine that a **People** would suddenly receive **Laws** that deprived them in some way of a freedom they had always possessed, or that they would be moved without resistance to condemn **Sacrifices** that carried the most religious pretext in the **World**, and to practice **Ceremonies** contrary to those they had seen practiced, and which they themselves had practiced, if they were not committed to it by some extraordinary motive?

It is then a matter that deserves to be considered with attention: the so exact description that **God** gives to **Moses** of the **Tabernacle** <note>The Tabernacle was a portable sanctuary used by the Israelites during their desert wanderings.</note>, of the parts of the **Ceremonial** worship that were attached to this sacred place, and of the manner in which this **Tabernacle**, its various **Parts**, its **Utensils**, and its **Ornaments** were completed.

But if it is easy to establish the **Divinity** of the **Laws of Moses** by showing the truth of the **Fact** concerning the manner in which **Moses** recounts that **God** gave them, one can gather the same truth by joining these **Reflections** to those that were made in the preceding **Chapters**.

### Regulations that had been made by Moses.

Now, who does not know how much a conqueror, such as Joshua, is usually jealous of being seen as the author of the regulations made in a state he founds, and how much he desires to have the glory for them? Who does not also know how a politician usually affects to combat the maxims of his predecessor, especially once a state changes its form of government, when power passes from one hand to another, and the state changes from a **Republic** to a **Monarchy**, as is seen among the **People of Israel**? <note>The author is referring to the historical shift in Israel from the era of the Judges—which early modern writers often compared to a Republic—to the establishment of the Kingship under Saul and David.</note> How is it that nothing of the sort ever happened in this state? How was there never found someone who affected to establish another **Jurisprudence** <term>Jurisprudence</term> <note>In this context, it refers to the entire system or philosophy of law governing the nation.</note>; to make regulations contrary to those that were under the name of **Moses**, if those who observed them had not been equally persuaded that they were **Divine**? How, among so many divisions, would there not have been someone who took care to disabuse the **Jews**, and to make them shake off the yoke of **Moses** and despise his **Authority**?

It is easy to prove by the whole course of the history of the **Jews**, that these **Laws**—which in the narrative of **Moses** are nested within one another, and whose occasions are represented as arising from the facts recited by **Moses**—were equally received by the **Jewish People**, having been compiled into a single body immediately before the death of **Moses**,

## CHAPTER VII.

### *That the accounts of Moses regarding the Oracles he recorded in these Books cannot be called into doubt.*

There are several kinds of Predictions in **Exodus** and in the following Books. First, there are those whose fulfillment was very swift, following the Prediction very closely.

The Deliverance of the **Jews** from slavery in **Egypt** was among these; **Moses** promises it, he predicts it: Indeed, he fulfills it himself; all the People who had been witnesses to this Prediction by **Moses** knew its fulfillment when they were delivered, and this is what established the Divinity of his Mission. <note>The author argues that because the Israelites saw the prophecy of their freedom and its immediate fulfillment through the Plagues and the Crossing of the Red Sea, they had empirical proof of Moses's divine authority.</note>

<margin>Deuteronomy 33</margin>
We see a similar Prediction regarding the Conquest of **Canaan**, and the manner of the divisions that were to be made; **Moses** expressly notes there the lot that was to fall to each of the **Tribes**, and designates the nature of the Country they were to occupy. <note>In Deuteronomy 33, Moses gives a prophetic blessing to each of the twelve tribes of Israel, which tradition holds described the specific geographic features of the lands they would eventually inherit in the Promised Land.</note>

The Posterity born in the **Desert** was witness to the Prediction; they subsequently saw its exact fulfillment, although one drew

It would be the most surprising thing in the world, this illusion: For one can notice a quite significant thing, which is that the Predictions of Moses are not hidden in some private Volume, but they are found inserted in the Orations <note>original: "Harangues," referring to the formal public speeches found in the Book of Deuteronomy</note> that he gave to all his People shortly before his death—Orations which were compiled into a single Body, to be a public Monument of his Ministry and his Prophecies in the midst of the Jews.

Now, there is quite a difference between a Book that is hidden, or which is not commonly read because it is composed entirely of Oracles—as the writings of the <term>Sibyl</term> <note>The Sibylline Books were a collection of oracular utterances in ancient Rome, kept secret by the state and consulted only in times of dire crisis</note> must have been—and a Book where the Oracles are mixed with a very diversified History that attracts and holds the Reader's eye; where they are mixed with Political and Ceremonial Laws, and where they are, so to speak, enmeshed among a great number of various events that serve as the occasion for the Revelations and Prophecies.

## CHAP. IX.

->*That it appears by the nature of the accounts that Moses*<-
->*provided in the Book of Genesis that he had*<-
->*the Messiah in view.*<-

I have shown in my Reflections on Genesis that those who preceded the time of Moses knew clearly that God was to raise up a Deliverer for them, although they did not have a clear knowledge of the manner in which he was to appear. I even believe I have established that the various ideas these Ancients formed regarding the first Promise <note>The "first Promise" refers to the theological concept of the *Protoevangelium* in Genesis 3:15, where God promises that the seed of the woman will crush the serpent's head</note> are what gave rise to most of the criminal actions of the <term>Patriarchs</term> <note>The early biblical fathers, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note>, or of those who held the same claim as they did <note>The author likely refers to instances where biblical figures took questionable actions, such as polygamy or deception, in a misguided attempt to ensure they would be the ancestors of the promised Savior</note>.

The task now is to show that Moses possessed the same knowledge as these Ancients. It is quite important to demonstrate this, so that it may not seem strange if I claim that most of the Laws published by this Founder of the Jewish Commonwealth <note>original: "République des Juifs"; in the 17th century, the term "Republic" or "Commonwealth" was commonly used to describe the organized theocratic state of ancient Israel</note>, most of the Regulations, whether Civil or Religious, that he made, and most of the <term>Oracles</term> <note>Prophetic divine pronouncements</note> he uttered—which were written down after him by the Prophets who lived within that Commonwealth of the

To make this easily understood, here are some general Reflections that one can justly form regarding the accounts Moses provided in Genesis.

First, it cannot be denied that Moses was one of the greatest and wisest Historians in the World: One cannot form a grander design than that of writing the History of twenty-four Centuries <note>Moses was traditionally credited with writing the first five books of the Bible, covering the timeline from the creation of the world (roughly 4000 BCE in traditional chronology) to his own time (roughly 1400 BCE)</note>. He treats the Creation of the World, the production of Man, his sin, the Promise God made to him after his sin, the Flood, and the Origin of all the Nations existing in his time.

Nor can one conceive of a grander manner than that in which he treats what he writes: He mingles a surprising force with a natural simplicity. He candidly <note>The original French "naïvement" here carries the older meaning of "naturally" or "without artifice," rather than "gullibly"</note> represents all the passions; he maintains the characters of the Men of whom he speaks, and of God himself. To be convinced of what I say, one need only read the History he gives of the Sacrifice of Abraham <note>The "Binding of Isaac" in Genesis 22, where Abraham is commanded to offer his son</note>, and the manner in which he describes the various emotions <note>original: "divers mouvemens," referring to the internal stirrings of the soul</note> of Joseph and his Brothers when they recognized one another. However, one can notice two quite surprising things in the Book of Genesis.

The first is that Moses relates things therein which seem unworthy of being recounted by an Historian as wise and as grave as he:

For example, he recounts exactly and in detail all the circumstances of the acquisition that Abraham made of the Cave of Machpelah <note>A site in Hebron purchased by Abraham to bury his wife Sarah; it became the family's ancestral tomb</note> to serve as a sepulcher for his wife. The other <note>referring to the second "surprising thing" mentioned at the end of the previous page</note> is that Moses reports a series of horrible crimes, which seem to blacken the memory of those whose actions he describes; a fairly large number of these are found in the Book of Genesis.

Now, it is unjust to suppose that Moses, being as wise as he appears to us, would have made so injudicious a choice of events—either of little importance, or altogether horrible and shocking—to fill a book as considerable as that in which he describes the Creation of the World, and where he constantly represents the Oracles of God. It is therefore natural to judge that he had his goal in view, namely that of the Promise <note>The "Promise" refers to the theological concept of a future Savior or Messiah promised to the patriarchs</note>, which sufficiently justifies the manner in which he entered into the account of these trivial things, or even these crimes.

Thus, in describing the acquisition of the **Cave of Machpelah** <note>The ancestral tomb in Hebron purchased by Abraham; the author argues this purchase was a legal claim to the Promised Land</note>, his goal was to show how God, through this sepulcher, wished to bind **Abraham** and his posterity to the **Land of Canaan**. In recounting the burial of **Rachel** at **Ephrath** <note>The ancient name for Bethlehem</note>, he had the same objective in mind.

Regarding the crimes that **Moses** describes, I have shown previously that his intention was to demonstrate how the hope of fulfilling the **Promise** <note>The "Promise" refers to the theological expectation of a coming Messiah or Savior, which the author believes motivated the actions of the Patriarchs</note> occupied the minds of the ancient faithful who preceded him. Indeed, one can say that **Moses’s** judgment and wisdom shine brilliantly in the choice he made of these facts to preserve their memory.

Let it not be said that it is a quite surprising thing to see that during such a long succession of centuries, the **Promise of the Messiah** produced nothing but wicked actions. For, besides the fact that it appears from all circumstances that the crimes **Moses** reports were not committed one after another, but only at very distant intervals, it is visible that **Moses** recounted these facts by design: namely, to show the impression that the knowledge of the **Promise of the Messiah** made on the minds of those who knew it perfectly.

In effect, if **Moses** had inserted into **Genesis** an ample account of what **Adam** might have first said on this matter on a **Sabbath** day, or if he had described what a **Seth**, an **Enoch**, and a **Shem** <note>Three key biblical patriarchs: Seth (Adam’s son), Enoch (who "walked with God"), and Shem (Noah’s son and ancestor of the Israelites)</note> might have said to their listeners

### IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS.

...preferring the reproach of the **Messiah** to the **Treasures of Egypt**, looking toward the reward. From where could such conduct—so far removed from what we see in ordinary men—originate, if not, as **Saint Paul** observed, from an entirely extraordinary source? That is to say, from the firm conviction he held that, however wretched the condition of the **People of Israel** was at that time, God had nevertheless chosen them to fulfill in their midst, in the fullness of time, that great **Promise**. This **Promise** had been the joy of their ancestors, and its concepts had been renewed and clarified by the **Oracles** that **Jacob** had pronounced on his deathbed.

A similar reflection must be made regarding the other action of **Moses**, who remained constantly devoted to the fate of this wretched people; he left **Egypt**, and returned only to lead the **Israelites** out of it, according to the orders God gave him. Indeed, all of this conduct consistently demonstrates a clear knowledge of the **Promises** God had made to this people, and a vivid conviction of the certainty of their fulfillment.

**Moses** described for us how he initially refused the calling addressed to him to lead the people out of the servitude they suffered in **Egypt**. He excused himself, in truth, by citing his weakness and his lack of ability. ## CHAP. XII.

->*That one sees in the Law of Moses a continuation of the plan that God had previously formed, by distinguishing those from whom he intended the Messiah to be born.*<-

IT was entirely necessary for my purpose to firmly establish the authority of the Books of Moses, upon which I intend to offer these Reflections. It was also necessary to show that Moses had a very clear knowledge of the Promise God made concerning a Deliverer <note>The Messiah</note>, which I believe I have proven quite solidly. Thus, I think I can now focus on showing that Moses and those who followed him had this Messiah in view in all their principal regulations.

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

But to provide greater clarity <note>The original French "lumiére" literally means light, used here in the sense of intellectual or spiritual enlightenment.</note> to the things I have to say, it seems to me that it would not be out of place to set before the reader’s eyes the nature of God’s conduct in this matter, so that one may better judge the progression of His design.

I have shown that in the Book of Genesis, Moses aimed to establish two things: first, that all men draw their origin from Adam, whom God created; second, that man having sinned, God promised to restore him through one of his descendants.

The first of these truths has been solidly established by factual proof, since I have shown that a tradition—such as the one Moses relates regarding the Creation—cannot be called into doubt.

As for the fact of the Promise concerning the **blessed seed** <term>blessed seed: a reference to the "seed of the woman" mentioned in Genesis 3:15, which theologians interpret as the first prophecy of a coming Messiah who would defeat evil</term>, which was the second but principal object of mankind's consolation and hope, I believe I have shown that the idea was very vivid, and that it is this very idea that gave rise to most of the extraordinary actions recounted by Moses.

But as this Promise was not to be fulfilled until many centuries later, God—who had expressed it in very general terms and without designating a time—reserved for Himself the task of explaining later both the manner in which it was to be fulfilled and the time when it would take effect; it is quite...

# IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS.

...altogether important to consider also how God preserved a distinct knowledge of it <note>The "it" refers to the Promise of the Savior/Seed mentioned on the previous page.</note>.

Now, in reading the reflections I have made on Genesis, one must have noticed that in these early times God gradually restricted to a few individuals this privilege of fulfilling this Promise and of giving birth to the Messiah, until finally He had invested it in the Tribe of Judah, as one reads in the forty-ninth chapter of Genesis.

In effect, one sees first that God restricted to the Family of Seth, to the exclusion of that of Ham <note>The author likely contrasts the line of Seth with that of Cain or the later cursed line of Ham; traditionally, Seth is viewed as the ancestor of the "sons of God."</note>, the privilege of fulfilling this Promise.

One sees that in the Family of Seth, He restricted this privilege to Noah alone and to his Family.

One sees that in the Family of Noah, He restricted it particularly to Shem, excluding his brothers from this advantage.

One sees that in the Family of Shem, He restricted it particularly to Abraham, to the exclusion of Lot and all the other descendants of Shem.

One sees that in the Family of Abraham, He chose Isaac, to the exclusion of Ishmael and his posterity.

One sees that between the children of Isaac, He preferred Jacob to Esau and his descendants.

Finally, between the children of Jacob, He chose Judah, to the exclusion of his brothers.

# REFLECTIONS ON THE

seven restrictions that God made, He seems to have purposefully chosen the **younger sons** <note>original: "cadets"; in the context of primogeniture, the choice of a younger son over the firstborn was a significant subversion of social norms.</note> rather than the **firstborn** to invest them with the right to fulfill the Promise. Seth was the younger brother of Cain; if one cannot say the same of Noah, nor of Shem who was the eldest of his brothers, Abraham is found to be the younger of the children of Terah, Isaac the younger than Ishmael, Jacob the younger than Esau, and Judah the younger of the children of Leah.

One could likewise, by judging God’s design by the outcome, correctly gather that God intended this choice to provoke continual jealousies from the firstborn—who thought that their birth gave them the right to the fulfillment of the Promise—against their younger brothers, who found themselves preferred over them by God’s choice.

One can also make this reflection: that it seems God wanted to strengthen these jealousies by sometimes creating a kind of conformity between the claimants to the execution of the Promise. For example, just as Abraham had two children, Lot had two; just as Jacob had twelve, Esau had twelve as well as Jacob; and sometimes it happens that the subjects He prefers are burdened with atrocious accusations <note>The author likely refers to the moral failings of the patriarchs, such as Jacob’s deception or Judah’s sins, suggesting that God’s choice is based on Grace or sovereign will rather than the personal merit of the individual.</note>.

After these general reflections, it is natural enough to consider how God took care to particularly distinguish those who found themselves invested with the right to fulfill the Promise, so that their status would not be uncertain.

->### IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS.<-

It was with the same view <note>original: "veuë"; meaning intention or perspective</note> that He drew Abraham from his native country, that He made him travel from place to place to make him famous, and to make him known to those who came to learn of his faith, his religion, and his hopes.

It was for this design in particular that He obliged him to practice **Circumcision**, which was a physical distinction and an indelible mark; that He fixed this Patriarch in a certain location by attaching him, in a way, to the **Cave of Machpelah** <note>The burial site of the patriarchs in Hebron. Finally, it was for the same reason that God distinguished the descendants of Jacob from all the other peoples of the Earth, by forbidding them from marrying among them and from imitating their worship and religion.

Now, all this being established, I say that—whether one considers the goal of the **Laws of Moses** and their primary use, or whether one reflects carefully on the **oracles** <note>prophetic revelations</note> that specifically characterize the **Messiah**—one finds that God has always remained within this spirit of distinction and in this design of fostering the "jealousy" <note>In this context, "jealousy" refers to a competitive vigilance or a zealous guarding of the right to the Promise.</note> of those who could claim the right to fulfill the **Promise**; that is to say, He followed the same plan until the time

...of Jesus Christ, in whom Christians maintain <note>original: "foûtiennent"; to hold or argue a position</note> that the first Promise has been fulfilled: God, on one hand, having then entirely ceased all those differences <note>The religious and legal distinctions that separated Jews from other nations.</note> which He had established only to ensure that the Messiah would be recognized with certainty—much like one takes down the scaffolding after the building has been brought to its perfection—and on the other, God having absolutely ended the principles by which this spirit of jealousy was maintained among those who held the same claim.

Indeed, we see that God excludes all other Peoples from the right to carry out the Promise. We see that within the Family of **Judah**, who was the youngest of the Children of **Leah** <note>Judah was Leah's fourth son; the author highlights how the divine choice often bypassed the firstborn sons (like Reuben) to create a specific, recognizable lineage.</note>, He restricted the fulfillment of the Promise to younger sons. We see that He allowed subjects of jealousy to arise even between the Tribes of the People of **Israel**. We see, finally, that none of the ways that can distinguish one People from among all the Peoples of the Earth, one Tribe among twelve Tribes, one Family within a Tribe, and one Person within a Family, has been omitted in following this plan and design.

This is what must be solidly established by examining the matter by degrees, from the time of **Moses**—whom God used to give the **Laws** by which one could surely know the Messiah—until the advent of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, in whom we claim the Promise of the Messiah has reached its fulfillment.

<summary>The author explains that the specific laws and tribal divisions of the Old Testament were temporary "scaffolding" designed to uniquely identify the Messiah, a process that concluded with the life of Jesus Christ. He argues that God intentionally chose specific lineages, such as the house of Judah, to make the messianic line unmistakable.</summary>

### CHAP. XIII.

->*That the manner in which God gave the Law to the*<-
->*People of Israel served to distinguish them from*<-
->*all other Peoples.*<-

**I** do not aim simply to first observe the consistencies <note>original: "conformités"</note> found between the things Moses recounts in Genesis and those found in the following Books.

Nor do I wish merely to establish that just as Jacob’s descent into Egypt with his children in the time of Joseph, and the protection they received there, served to fulfill the Prophecy <note>original: "Prédiction"</note> that God had made of this event through the dreams He sent to Joseph; one can say in general that the sending of Moses, his Miracles, and his entire Ministry until his death—where he entrusted the leadership of the Jews to Joshua—is a literal fulfillment of the first part of the Promises God had made to Abraham: to deliver his fourth Generation <note>In Genesis 15:16, God tells Abraham his descendants will return to Canaan in the "fourth generation."</note> from the captivity into which it was to fall, and to lead it into the Land of Canaan to possess it.

It is true that this People, according to the terms of the Oracle <note>A reference to divine revelation or prophecy.</note>, is described to us, first by Moses, as groaning in a foreign Land—

But that is not all that makes both the Person and the Ministry of Moses so glorious. There is something in the Books of Exodus and in the following ones, of which Moses is the Author, that deserves much more consideration.

### IV. THE SUBSEQUENT BOOKS.

...ple of the Jews. And this is the primary perspective I must maintain while making my Reflections on Exodus and on the subsequent Books.

Generally speaking, it can be said that this People was already distinguished by the Sabbath and by Circumcision. However, as I have shown that the Sabbath was a Law common to all the Peoples of the Earth, and that Circumcision was common to the Ishmaelites and the Edomites <note>original: "Iduméens"; the descendants of Esau who lived in the region of Idumea.</note>—which requires us to make specific reflections on the matter—we must consider how God took care to distinguish this People by giving them His Law.

It is generally evident that the care God took in giving the Moral Law to the entire People was a singular distinction He made between this People and the other Nations, whom He "allowed to walk in their own ways," as Saint Paul says <note>A reference to Acts 14:16 in the New Testament.</note>. He wished to bind this People to Himself and prevent them from imitating the Nations in their Idolatry and in the excesses of vice that lead to the ruin of Society. Without doubt, the astonishing display <note>original: "appareil"; referring to the awe-inspiring and terrifying physical manifestations, such as thunder and fire, that accompanied the giving of the Law on Mount Sinai.</note> with which He gave this Law—a display that God had recorded in the presence of those who had been its witnesses—must have contributed to making them view their Nation as one that God loved particularly, and which He honored with a very special guidance.

The same reflection should be made regarding the Political Law that God was pleased to give them, the care He wished to take in regulating their governance <note>original: "Police"; in this historical context, the term refers to the civil administration, public order, and the general organization of the state.</note>, and in preventing the

difficulties <meta>catchword: difficultés</meta>

If he went hunting, He commanded him to pour out the blood and cover it with dust; if he planted, He forbade him the fruits of the first three years, treating them as the foreskin of the trees <note>original: "prépuce des arbres"; a literal translation of the Hebrew term *orlah*, referring to the law in Leviticus 19:23 which forbids eating fruit from a newly planted tree for three years.</note>; if he found a grave, He prescribed certain precautions; if he shaved his beard, He forbade him from cutting the edges of his beard; if he built a house, He required it to have parapets <note>original: "gardefoux"; guardrails or low walls required on flat roofs by Deuteronomy 22:8 to prevent accidents.</note>, and that he place <term>mezuzahs</term> <note>original: "mézouzoth"; small parchment scrolls inscribed with Torah verses, placed in decorative cases on doorposts.</note> upon it. But if it can be said in general that the whole purpose of the **Ceremonial Law** was to keep the Jews occupied, it can be said even more accurately that its purpose was to inspire in them a horror of **Idolatry**, and an aversion to communion with **Idolaters**: And this is recognized when one enters into a more detailed examination of these Laws.

This is seen perfectly in the manner in which God declares that some meats were impure, and in which He forbids certain animals. For it happens that most of these animals were considered symbols of Divinity, and that they were offered religious worship among the **Pagans**. One of the most learned Jews who has written has carefully remarked that God gave many **Ceremonial Laws** directly opposed to those that the **Sabians** <note>An ancient group of people from Mesopotamia whom Maimonides identified as the quintessential idolaters of Abraham's time.</note> practiced in their superstitious worship; this is something a learned English Doctor <note>Likely John Spencer (1630–1693), a pioneer in the study of comparative religion, who expanded on Maimonides' ideas in his work *On the Ritual Laws of the Hebrews*.</note> has just amply proven regarding the rites God prescribed in the **Sacrifice of the Paschal Lamb**, the manner in which the **Altar** was to be built and how one was to ascend it, the prohibition against cooking a **kid** in its mother’s milk; the prohibition against offering honey in **Sacrifices**; the prohibition against passing children through the fire in honor of **Moloch**; the **Law** which forbade eating blood; the Law which forbade cutting hair in a circle; the prohibition against making **marks** <note>original: "stigmates"; referring to the prohibition of tattoos or ritual scarring found in Leviticus 19:28.</note> in the skin; and I know not how many Laws of this nature, the purpose of which does not appear so clear today now that the rites of these ancient **Idolaters** are completely abolished, the memory of them barely surviving in a few ancient Authors.

It takes only a little **Reflection** on this conduct of God in giving the Laws to his People, to judge that the great design of these Laws was to put a barrier between the **People of Israel** and the **Idolatrous Nations**;

# CHAPTER XIV.

## *That God seems to have wanted to maintain the spirit of jealousy within the heart of the Jewish Nation.*

IT is seen in general that God, in giving His **Law** <note>Refers to the Torah or the Mosaic Law.</note> to the **People of Israel**, aimed to distinguish this People from all other Nations of the World, and in particular from those Nations who were jealous of them; I mean the **Moabites**, the **Ammonites**, the **Ishmaelites**, and the **Idumeans** <note>These groups were all traditionally related to the Israelites through common ancestors (Lot, Ishmael, and Esau) and lived in the regions surrounding ancient Israel, often in a state of conflict or rivalry.</note>, as I shall explain in what follows. But I have also remarked that God had excited, through His conduct and His **Oracles** <note>Divine pronouncements or prophecies.</note>, a spirit of jealousy among these various claimants to the **Promise** <note>The "Promise" refers to the biblical covenant made with Abraham regarding his descendants and the eventual coming of a savior.</note>. It is therefore necessary to show now that God followed the same plan in the **Law**, and that He used this spirit of jealousy as another means to ensure that the ideas of the **Messiah** were always extremely vivid among this Nation which He thus distinguished from all other Peoples of the World. It will perhaps seem to the Reader that these **Reflections**

<summary>This page argues that God intentionally fostered a sense of distinction and even "jealousy" between the Israelites and neighboring tribes like the Moabites and Edomites to preserve the Jewish identity and the expectation of the Messiah.</summary>
<keywords>Messiah, Law of Moses, People of Israel, Moabites, Ammonites, Ishmaelites, Idumeans, Divine Promise, religious identity</keywords>

<header>REFLECTIONS ON THE</header>

reflections interrupt the considerations I must make on the **Law of Moses** <note>Also known as the Torah, the first five books of the Bible.</note>: But besides the fact that I will resume them shortly, it will be easily perceived that these two ideas—the spirit of distinction and the spirit of jealousy within the heart of this Nation—must always be noted, if one wishes to know the spirit of God's Laws and the principles of the events reported to us by **Moses** and by the sacred Authors who acted according to the spirit of the Laws that Moses had given to the **People of Israel**.

I begin with two general Reflections which seem to me entirely important for explaining this conduct of God.

One may have noticed two things that served as a pretext for maintaining the jealousy of which Moses gave so many examples in the **Book of Genesis**.

The first is the preference that God ordinarily showed for the **Younger sons** <term>Cadets: In the context of biblical primogeniture, the younger brothers were often chosen by God over the firstborn (Aînés), such as Jacob over Esau.</term> over the **Eldest**.

The second is the choice of certain persons who must have appeared altogether unworthy of God's choice.

Now, this is something of which one sees a continual sequence in the **History of the Jews** described by Moses, and by the Prophets who made this observation based on the model that Moses had provided.

Firstly, the **Tribe of Judah** is found to be preferred over all the **Tribes of Israel**, and in particular over that of **Ruben** <note>Reuben was the firstborn son of the patriarch Jacob, but lost his birthright, which eventually led to the leadership of the nation being associated with the tribe of Judah.</note>, which

What likelihood, they might say, is there that after **Moses**, by the command of the **Eternal**, put to death those **Israelites** who had relations with the **daughters of Moab** following the oracle of **Balaam** <note>Referring to the events at Shittim (Numbers 25), where Israelites were led into idolatry and immorality by Moabite women.</note>, God would have wished to bring forth the **Messiah** by means of a **Moabite** woman? ### CHAP. XV.

#### *That Circumcision was a means of distinguishing the People of Israel from the other Nations.*

But we must now resume our considerations regarding the Laws of Moses. Circumcision was undoubtedly one of the first and most noticeable means by which God distinguished the descendants of Israel from all other peoples of the Earth. It is therefore upon this that I must first fix my attention.

<margin>History, book 5</margin>
One need only consider it at first glance to recognize that this distinction was real and physical. Indeed, Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD) was a Roman historian. In his "Histories," he wrote extensively, and often critically, about Jewish customs from a Roman perspective.</note> says: *The Jews instituted the practice of circumcising the genitals so that they might be recognized by their difference* <note>original Latin: "Circuncidere genitalia instituêre Judæi ut diversitate noscantur"</note>; But it also ensured that the Covenant of God <note>The "Covenant" refers to the sacred agreement between God and the Israelites, initiated with Abraham.</note> was, as it were, engraved and imprinted into the flesh of all the posterity of Abraham.

God explained Himself thus in various places, and one can say that this was entirely in accordance with His design, since He wished to prevent this people, from whom the Messiah was to be born, from coming to mingle with the other peoples of the Earth—which would have rendered the origin of the Messiah suspect, or at least

I will not recount here the various views of theologians to explain the purpose of this ceremony; but I believe I should make two quite natural reflections upon it.

The first is that it was primarily with the promised Messiah in view that God willed this mark to be in the part used for generation <note>This refers to the reproductive organs, emphasizing the physical lineage of the promised Savior.</note>. Since the Messiah was to be born through generation according to the terms of the first Oracle <note>The "first Oracle" refers to the "Protoevangelium" in Genesis 3:15, the first prophecy of a savior who would be the "seed" of the woman.</note>, and according to the clarifications God had given to Abraham, what could be more in keeping with the hope and the idea of the Messiah than to distinguish this People by their relation to that blessed seed <term>Seed: In biblical context, this refers to the offspring or biological descendants who would fulfill God's promise.</term> He promised them? No doubt, just as He wanted to oblige the Jews to keep before their eyes the first Promise made to the Human Race, He also wanted to fix their minds on the grace He had shown them, as well as to Abraham, in distinguishing them from all other Peoples of the Earth, so that the Deliverer might be born within the heart of the Republic <note>The author uses "Republic" in the classical sense to describe the organized body politic and social structure of the ancient Israelite nation.</note>, and within the bounds of their Posterity.

The second Reflection is that, with this view in mind, God subjected those among the People of Israel who remained uncircumcised, or who left their Children uncircumcised, to the penalty of death.

Is it not an altogether surprising thing, this rigor for the practice of a Ceremony which had in itself no

Now, I ask you, what was the use of all these distinctions, if they did not aim to serve in making the **Messiah** <note>The "Anointed One" promised in Jewish scripture.</note> known with certainty when He would be manifested?

In fact, one sees that this was solely their purpose; God had resolved to have the **Messiah** born from the **Tribe of Judah**, so it was therefore necessary that the **Genealogies of the Tribe of Judah** be very well known.

This is also what we see God brought about, not only with as much care as was necessary, but even with greater care than one could reasonably ask. He engaged all the **Tribes**, and this **People**, in a kind of devotion toward preserving their **Genealogical lists**, and He overlooked none of the means that could serve to make the **Tribe of Judah** known among the other **Tribes**, and the **Family** that was to give birth to the **Messiah** known among the other **Families** of the **Tribe of Judah**.

### IV. SUBSEQUENT BOOKS

The second Remark is that, from this perspective, the sacred Books of the **Old Testament** are filled with **Genealogical Lists**. What purpose would these **Genealogies** otherwise serve in Books of such importance, in Books that deal with such grand subjects? **Moses** <note>Moses is traditionally credited with writing the first five books of the Bible (the Pentateuch), which contain extensive lineages, such as those in Genesis and Numbers.</note> fills almost all of his Works with them; the first Book of **Chronicles** summarizes these **Genealogies** with exacting care. The Book of **Ruth** seems to have nothing of greater significance than the **Genealogy of David** and of his **Family**.

The third Remark is that while it appears the **Genealogy of the Priests** was better known than those of other Families—because they were the public Ministers of Religion, whereby the **Genealogies** of the **Tribe of Levi** <note>The Tribe of Levi was set apart for religious duties, with the family of Aaron serving as priests; their lineage was strictly monitored to maintain the sanctity of the Temple service.</note> seem to have had more prominence than those of **Judah**—one finds nonetheless that the care to preserve one’s **Genealogy** was roughly equal throughout the entire nation of the **Jews**.

Indeed, if upon the return from the **Babylonian Captivity** <note>The Babylonian Captivity (6th century BCE) was a pivotal era where the Jewish people were exiled; upon their return, records were used to re-establish land rights and religious roles.</note> certain persons were excluded from the order of the **Priesthood** to which they laid claim because they could not justify their parentage, those from other **Tribes** who did not clearly prove their **Genealogy** were likewise rejected.

This is because **God**, desiring that within the **Family of Judah**, the **Family of David**—from whom He intended the **Messiah** to be born—be even more specifically known, raised **David** to the glory of **Kingship**.

Everyone knows that in a State, the **Genealogy** of a King and of those descended from him is the one that is most widely known. If **Royal Blood** is always distinguished, how much more so if this worldly elevation serves to make known a **Family** as the one **God** particularly chose for the birth of Him who is marked as the "Expectation of all Nations"? <note>This phrase refers to a common Messianic title derived from Haggai 2:7, emphasizing the global significance of the promised Savior.</note>

<margin>Isaiah 11</margin>
I boldly declare that **God** raised the **Family of David** to the **Throne** only with the purpose I have just described, since, in fact, the **Messiah** was to be born from the **Family of David** reduced to a particular state. This is what **Isaiah** expresses by saying He was to be born from the "stem of Jesse" <note>original: "tronc d'Esaï." Jesse was the father of King David; the prophecy suggests a new branch growing from a cut-down tree, symbolizing the Messiah rising from a diminished royal line.</note>, as this was also very consistent with the character of a **Prophet**, and with the state of self-emptying <term>anéantissement: a theological concept describing the humble, lowly human condition in which Christ appeared, often called kenosis</term> in which **our Lord** was to appear in the world.

## CHAP. XVII.

->*Response to some difficulties regarding the matter of Genealogies.*<-

It seems, in truth, that the dispersal <note>original: "dissipation." The author refers to the scattering of the ten northern tribes of Israel following the Assyrian conquest.</note> of several Tribes provides a very natural objection to those who would wish to dispute the reflections I have made regarding these **Genealogies**.

But fundamentally, one can find in this very event the means to establish them with even greater solidity.

Indeed, one might say that the total dispersal of the **Tribe of Ephraim**, and of those who had joined it in the revolt of **Jeroboam** <note>Jeroboam led the rebellion that split Israel into two kingdoms: the Northern Kingdom (Israel) and the Southern Kingdom (Judah).</note>, is a **Mystery of Providence**, as worthy of admiration as the elevation of the **Family** from which the **Messiah** was to be born to the glory of **Kingship**.

To understand this well, one must keep before one's eyes the claims of the **Tribe of Ephraim**. **Ephraim** was the younger son of **Manasseh**; but **Jacob** had granted him the **Birthright** <note>original: "Primogeniture." This refers to the right of the eldest son to a double inheritance and the leadership of the family.</note>, much as **Isaac** had given it to **Jacob**, even though it belonged to **Esau**, who was the elder. **Jacob** had specifically blessed **Ephraim**; he had even turned it into a standard formula of blessing, much as one reads in the...

What then does the Lord do to confront this claim of the Tribe of Ephraim? ## CHAP. XVIII.

### *That the manner in which the Law of Moses attached the People of Israel to the Land of Canaan was intended to distinguish them from other nations.*

Here is another of the primary means that God employed to distinguish the people of Israel from all other peoples of the Earth: It was to attach the affections of this people to the Land of Canaan, in such a way that they would regard it as a country belonging to them by virtue of a grant from God. This grant overrode the division made among the children of Noah <note>Referring to the biblical "Table of Nations" in the Book of Genesis, where the world was divided among the descendants of Noah after the Flood.</note>, which had assigned it to the Canaanites, or which

But in fact, God—who wished to preserve the Gibeonites <note>The Gibeonites (Gabaonites) were a group of Hivites who, according to the Book of Joshua, tricked the Israelites into a peace treaty. Though they were spared from the general destruction, they were forced into perpetual service.</note> among the Jews, and even had them assigned to serve the duties of the Tabernacle <note>The Tabernacle was the portable earthly dwelling place of God used by the Israelites from the time of the Exodus until the building of Solomon's Temple.</note>—God, I say, allowed it only to preserve irreproachable witnesses to the ancient claims of the Hebrews, and subsequently to the miraculous Conquest they made of the Land of Canaan. The survival of the Gibeonites among the Jews in the heart of the Country, and their employment in carrying wood and water for the service of the Tabernacle—although they were initially of another Religion—was, as I have said, a continual subject of wonder and a matter of questioning for Posterity, whether on the part of the Jews or on the part of the Gibeonites themselves.

# IV. FOLLOWING BOOKS.

...to find themselves together at the three solemn **Feasts**, which hardly allowed them to leave the **Country**, and which obliged them to renew together—through the public acts of **Religion**—the memory and the ideas of their hopes. This is how these festivals served God’s design.

These **Feasts** were celebrated at three times of the year that were most convenient for travel, namely between the months of March and October: **Passover** <note>original: "La Pâque"; the festival commemorating the Exodus from Egypt, occurring in early spring.</note> was celebrated on the fourteenth day of the March moon; **Pentecost** <note>original: "la Pentecôte"; also known as the Feast of Weeks, occurring seven weeks after Passover.</note> fifty days later; and the **Feast of Propitiations** <note>original: "Fête des Propitiations"; this refers to the period of the High Holy Days, specifically the ten days from Rosh Hashanah (the New Year) to Yom Kippur (the Day of Atonement).</note> from the first day until the tenth of September.

Furthermore, the Law that obliged the People to be present was so rigorous regarding males of twenty years and older that there was no exemption; all, without exception, were required to attend. And indeed, even during the periods of dispersal, one saw the Jews living outside of Judea return for these three Feasts with great care.

It was visibly a means of keeping the **Tribes** of the **People of Israel** distinct by giving each of them a separate portion in the **Land of Canaan**, and attaching them to it inseparably—just as it was a means of distinguishing the whole People by giving them a country that was entirely separate.

This is also the method we see that God followed in giving a Law that commands the division of the **Land of Canaan** into a

I do not note here the wonder that occurred in this division—namely, that it was precisely an exact fulfillment of the Oracle pronounced by **Jacob** and by **Moses** shortly before their death <note>This refers to the prophetic blessings found in Genesis 49 and Deuteronomy 33, which foretold the character and territory of each tribe.</note>.

But I note, firstly, that since God’s intention was to distinguish this People into **Tribes**, so that the **Tribe of Judah** <note>original: "Juda"</note>, from which the **Messiah** was to be born, would remain more known and distinct, He caused both the **Cave of Machpelah** <note>original: "Caverne de Macpela"; the sacred burial site of the Patriarchs Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in Hebron.</note>, where all the **Patriarchs** were buried, and the place of **Ephrath** where **Rachel** was interred—that is to say, the place which was later called **Bethlehem**, the homeland of **David**, the place marked by **Micah** <note>original: "Michée"</note> the Prophet under the name of **Ephrath**, the place where the **Messiah** was to be born in the fullness of time—to fall into the portion of **Judah**.

Next, one must note two particular regulations that God had established to attach His People to the **Land of Canaan**, and to maintain the distinct idea of this first division of the Country.

The first is the **Law of the Jubilee** <note>original: "Loi du Jubilé"; a sacred year occurring every fifty years when slaves were freed and land was returned to its original ancestral owners.</note>, and the second is the **Law of Lineal Retraction** <term>Lineal Retraction (Retraits Lignagers): A legal right allowing a family member to reclaim or buy back ancestral land sold to an outsider, ensuring the property remained within the original family lineage.</term>.

Indeed, by the **Law of the Jubilee**, which...

Thus, it would have been in vain to alienate the **Field of Ephrath** <note>original: "Champ d'Efrat"; a region associated with Bethlehem and the burial place of Rachel.</note>; it always returned to the **Family of Judah**, and it subsequently always returned to the **Family of David**, to whose ancestors God had caused it to fall.

This **Law of the Jubilee** served to preserve the distinct identity of the **Tribes** and the **Families**; it also served to preserve the distinct record of the **Genealogies**, which it was necessary to maintain in order to preserve one’s right to the inheritance of one's predecessors.

One might pointlessly object to this observation by claiming that it was impossible to know for certain if a possession belonged to a **Tribe**, and even less to a **Family**, since one sees that the first **Proselytes** <note>original: "Prosélytés"; converts to Judaism who joined the Israelites during the Exodus.</note> who departed with **Israel** shared the **Land** with them. Two other Laws also contributed to this goal: one regulated the manner in which one should act when only daughters remained in a household, regarding which the history of the **Daughters of Zelophehad** <note>In the Book of Numbers, the five daughters of Zelophehad successfully argued that they should inherit their deceased father's land since he had no sons, leading to a specific legal ruling by Moses.</note> is particularly significant. **Moses** recounted it twice with this view, as we shall explain in detail elsewhere.
