### CHAPTER XX.

*That the Laws of Moses regarding the State of Virginity primarily concerned the Messiah, which is also the case for various other Laws.*

So far, I have demonstrated the care God took to distinguish the **People of Israel** from all the other peoples of the Earth; I then showed the care He took to maintain the distinction of this People into **Tribes**. Following that, I showed the Laws He gave to ensure that the **Families** of each Tribe remained distinct. I also showed how the division of the **Land of Canaan** was a very suitable means to carry out this design of God. All of this shows that God intended for it to be easy to distinguish the **Messiah**, once He had explained that He would cause Him to be born from the **Family of Jesse** <note>original: "Isai." Jesse was the father of King David; biblical prophecy held that the Messiah would be a descendant of this line.</note>, as He did subsequently through various precise **Oracles** <note>In this context, "oracles" refers to divine prophecies or pronouncements delivered by the prophets.</note>.

But as there was to be something supernatural in the birth of the Messiah, who was to emerge from a **Virgin**, it is necessary to see how God took care in these Laws to ensure that the birth of the Messiah was not, on this point, a fact too difficult to recognize and establish.

Finally, one sees that God had even cast a stain upon the **Lepers**, because they were considered to have been conceived at a time when women had their maladies <note>A common historical euphemism for the menstrual cycle.</note>; it is upon this that the Law of the Lepers is founded, which deprived them of the right ever to enter the assembly of His People.

The same observation must be made regarding those who were born **illegitimate**, for they were deprived of the right to enter the assembly; and as innocent as they were of the debauchery of those who had given them life, they nonetheless shared in the punishment for their sin.

If one reflects attentively on all these Laws, one will find on one hand that some are of very slight consequence, unless one supposes that God had this aim of reliably distinguishing the state of virginity.

On the other hand, one will find that there are some very rigorous ones concerning matters that seem not to merit such terrible **severity**.

### IV. THE FOLLOWING BOOKS

But I am certain that they <note>Referring to the seemingly rigorous or trivial laws discussed on the previous page, such as the laws regarding leprosy and illegitimacy.</note> will appear very worthy of the wisdom of the **Legislator** <note>The "Legislator" refers to God as the author of the laws given to Moses.</note>, if they are considered within God's design, as He subsequently explained it to us in detail.

One need only apply one’s mind to the method of God which I have just explained, to recognize that nothing can be conceived as better directed than this plan, within the design that God had formed.

One could make the same reflections, and examine the same features of wisdom in the other precautions God had taken to reliably distinguish the **Messiah**, according to the various characteristics under which He was to present him thereafter through the ministry of the **Prophets**.

We see that since the Messiah was to be born of a Virgin, he had to be a **first-born**, and we find many Laws that generally distinguish the status of the <term>first-born: "prémier-né," referring to the special legal and religious status of the eldest male child, who was often dedicated to God's service.</term>.

In this view, God provides, through the ministry of **Moses**, certain characteristics to distinguish true Prophets from those who strove to usurp the name.

It appears likewise that God was to communicate to the Messiah the power to perform **Miracles**; this is what Moses explains by saying

## CHAP. XXI.

*That God's purpose in the Ceremonies and in the devotion He inspired in the Jews for the Tabernacle was to make them preserve the ideas of the Messiah whom He had promised, by distinguishing them from other Nations.*

After all the reflections I have just made on the different Laws that God employed to execute the design He had of binding the People of **Israel** to a certain place, and distinguishing them there; it is quite natural to judge that He had the same goal in the **Ceremonial Laws** which particularly determine the outward form of Religion.

This is what must be demonstrated, both regarding all the holiness that God attributes to the **Land of Canaan**, and regarding the entire Ministry of the **Tabernacle**, with its deeply mysterious construction, and subsequently the construction of a material **Temple** intended to serve as the center of all **Religion**.

It is indeed with this view <note>Referring to the "great design" of distinguishing the Israelites to preserve the lineage and promise of the Messiah.</note> that He had particularly consecrated several things, such as the <term>first fruits<gloss>the first agricultural produce of a season, offered to God</gloss></term>, the firstborn, and that He had consecrated certain times—for example, the first of the month and certain solemn Festivals—in addition to the day of the **Sabbath** which had been celebrated since earlier times. It is with the same view that He had particularly consecrated certain places, such as the **Cities of Refuge** <note>Six cities designated in the Land of Israel where those who committed manslaughter could claim asylum.</note>, and even more particularly the **Tabernacle** and the **Temple**; and above all, that He had consecrated certain **Rites** and **Ceremonies**, certain **Sacrifices** of living and inanimate things, and certain offerings of gold and silver, of incense, etc.

One sees that He rejected the first fruits if they were fruits grown outside the **Land of Canaan**; one sees that even though there were some **Sacrifices** common to the **Jews** and their neighboring Peoples, He nevertheless distinguished them by so many circumstances that they differed from those of their Neighbors. It is thus that He forbids them from using **Statues** <note>original: "Statuës"; referring here to cultic idols.</note>, before which people sacrificed in those days, and from sacrificing on <term>high places<gloss>elevated sites or hilltop altars used for worship, often associated with pagan rituals</gloss></term>, where the custom of sacrificing from the point

It should even be noted that after having fixed the **Temple** at **Jerusalem**, and having it succeed the **Tabernacle**—whose glory passed to the **Temple**—He willed it to be such a tangible mark of His presence that, in re-reading the march of **Israel** in the **Desert**, one finds that the building of the **Temple** of **Jerusalem** was an image and an expression of it. Just as the **Tabernacle** had been seen in the middle of the Camp of the **Levites**, and the **Levites** in the middle of the **Tribes** arranged each under its standard, so too were seen the **Temple**, the **Levites**, and the **People** of **Jerusalem** placed according to different degrees of **holiness** as one approached the **Most Holy Place** where the dwelling of **Holiness** resided.

Certainly, one could say that this design of building a **Tabernacle** or a **Temple** appears directly contrary to the spirit of **Religion**, according to which God, being spirit, wishes to be worshipped *in Spirit and in truth* <note>original: "en Esprit & en verité" — an allusion to John 4:24</note>. He seems, in effect, to depart for a time from this **Spirit** by ordering the construction of a **Tabernacle**.

### CHAP. XXII.

->*That it appears through the Books of Moses that the entire plan God had formed was intended to last only until the coming of the Messiah.*<-

But if one finds that in the Law of Moses there is a very narrow restriction regarding the execution of the Promise of the Messiah <note>The "Promise" refers to the theological concept of the protoevangelium or the first gospel, where God promised a savior to humanity.</note> within the borders of the Jewish Nation; which shows that God wished to distinguish them in this regard from all other Nations of the World, until the blessed Seed <note>original: "Semence bénite." A biblical term for the descendant of Abraham who would bless all nations (Genesis 22:18).</note> had arrived; if one finds that He wished to restrict this Promise to a single Tribe, to a single City, to a specific Family within that City, and to a very distinguished Person within that Family, so that one would not have to seek Him among the confused crowd of an entire People composed of several Million Persons, or within the heart of an entire Tribe, or in the vastness of a city filled with various Families: If with this view He restricted His birth to one People, to one Tribe, to one small City, and to one Family established there, the Jews could perceive therein God’s design, which was to eventually break all these restrictions,

The author suggests that these limitations were a deliberate divine strategy to prevent confusion among the millions of people in the Jewish nation.</summary>

Firstly, God had expressed the first Promise in very general terms, which contained the idea of a benefit common to all the Posterity of Adam. This Promise <note>Refers to the *protoevangelium* in Genesis 3:15, the first gospel promise made to Adam and Eve.</note> had been made before God had rejected the Posterity of Cain in favor of Seth; this Promise had been made before He had restricted it within Seth's line to the person of Noah, and subsequently to that of Shem within the Family of Noah.

In the second place, God, who by Circumcision had distinguished Abraham and his Posterity within the Family of Shem, expressly declared to Abraham that in his seed all the Nations of the Earth would be blessed: This Promise was made before God had restricted the Promise to the Person of Isaac to the detriment of Ishmael, and before another restriction was made to the Person of Jacob to the detriment of Esau.

In the third place, we see the same truth explained even more clearly in the Oracle concerning Judah in the forty-ninth chapter of Genesis: there, Jacob expressly predicts that the Messiah would be the *expectation of the Nations*, <note>original: "l’attente des Nations." [blessi]ngs that their entry into the Holy Land <term>Holy Land: The land of Israel, promised to the descendants of Abraham, where the Israelites were meant to remain distinct from other nations.</term> was intended to maintain—since they were to be separated there from the rest of the Earth—were not intended to last forever.

But this appears particularly in the Law of Moses <term>Law of Moses: The Torah, or the first five books of the Bible, containing the legal and religious framework for Israel.</term>. For, in the first place, it establishes different kinds of Proselytes <term>Proselytes: From the Greek *proselytos*, meaning a "newcomer." These were converts to Judaism; some followed all laws, while others, known as "Proselytes of the Gate," followed only the basic moral laws of Noah.</term>; it admits those who were not obliged to observe all the Ceremonies of the Law. Why this distinction, if it were absolutely necessary to become a Jew to share in the fruits of the Promise?

Secondly, it supposes that God was to transport the People into countries far from their own, even to the ends of the World. In the design of Providence, this was done only to make the Promise of the Messiah <term>Messiah: The "Anointed One" (from the Hebrew *Mashiach*) promised by God to bring salvation and restoration.</term> widely known, to strengthen belief in the Prophets, and to facilitate the Calling of the Gentiles <term>Gentiles: A term used to refer to all non-Jewish nations and peoples.</term> to the Faith.

In the third place, he expressly declares—as Saint Paul noted <note>referencing Romans 10:19, where Paul quotes Deuteronomy 32:21: "I will move them to jealousy with those which are not a people."</note>—that He was to call His People those who were not His People, and thus excite the jealousy of the People of Israel. This idea of jealousy is the most significant matter in Saint Paul's expression, if one considers that spirit of jealousy which reigned between Israel and neighboring Peoples.

### REFLECTIONS ON THE

[...warn] the pagans of the right that all Peoples, as well as the Jews, had to this Promise, so that they might take part in it when they were invited to do so on God’s behalf. Above all, this was to remove the scandal <note>In this context, "scandal" refers to a stumbling block or a cause for doubt and shock to one's faith.</note> that was bound to arise when God would come to destroy the form of this Republic <note>The "Jewish Republic" refers to the sovereign Jewish state or commonwealth as established by the Mosaic Law.</note> by means of the Romans, according to what He had predicted through Daniel.

All these things undoubtedly obliged the Jews who read the Books of Moses to make specific Reflections upon them.

But they were, above all, to notice three things. The first was that although God had initially chosen the entire People, He had nevertheless dispersed nearly ten out of thirteen parts <note>The author refers to the twelve tribes of Israel (sometimes counted as thirteen when Joseph's sons, Ephraim and Manasseh, are listed separately). The "ten parts" refers to the ten tribes of the northern kingdom of Israel carried away by the Assyrians.</note>. This showed clearly enough that the choice He had made of them was only an economic <term>economic: Derived from the Greek "oikonomia," this refers to God’s strategic plan or "management" of human history for the purpose of salvation, rather than financial matters.</term> choice, and only for a specific purpose.

The Second thing was that although He recommended the Ceremonial service with much exactness and severity, He had nevertheless fixed most of its observations to certain places, certain times, and certain people. It was easy to judge that God, by limiting it thus, did not regard it as anything other than an economic service, prescribed for specific aims, and which was thus destined to come to an end one day.

The third thing is that experience taught them that God, who seemed to have fixed His service to the Temple, nevertheless permitted its ruin by the hand of the Chaldeans <note>The Babylonians, led by Nebuchadnezzar, who destroyed the First Temple in 586 BCE.</note>, and that by having it re-

->THE END.<-

These are the principal Reflections I have made on the five Books of Moses, which may be regarded as the solid foundations of the Christian religion.
